## Page 3


Abstract
In this thesis we define n-duals of VB n-groupoids over Lie n-groupoids and
study their properties. For n = 0 this returns the dual vector bundle construc-
tion, while for n = 1 this returns Pradines’s construction of the dual of a VB
groupoid over a Lie groupoid, which includes the cotangent symplectic group-
oid of Coste, Dazord and Weinstein. For n = 2, we propose a new construction
that shows that VB 2-duals exist for VB 2-groupoids and they are VB 2-groupoids
themselves. Their canonical dual pairings are nondegenerate up to homotopy in
the same sense as shifted symplectic structures.
In particular, we can apply this construction to the tangent of a Lie 2-groupoid
and obtain a cotangent VB 2-groupoid (the 2-cotangent) which is canonically 2-
shifted symplectic. We apply this in two ways: First, to characterize 2-shifted
symplectic structures on a Lie 2-groupoid as Morita equivalences between its
tangent and 2-cotangent groupoid. Second, to compute the 2-cotangent of a Lie
1-groupoid and show it is symplectic Morita equivalent to the bar construction
of the 1-cotangent.
Along the way, we develop the theory of n-duals for simplicial vector spaces,
which covers the case where the base is a point. In this case, n-duals always
exist, as they are defined by a mapping space construction. By a reformulation
of the Eilenberg-Zilber theorem in terms of mapping spaces, we obtain that the
canonical n-dual pairing is nondegenerate up to homotopy for all n-types.
Keywords: Duality, higher vector bundles, Lie n-groupoids, shifted symplectic
structures, higher vector spaces, Eilenberg-Zilber theorem
DOI: doi:10.53846/goediss-11918

Let E and F be vector bundles over the same base manifold M. A pairing of vector
bundles between E and F is a bundle map
E ⊗F −→R × M,
over the identity on M.1 Every vector bundle E over a smooth manifold M has a
dual vector bundle E∗, which can be defined by the following universal property:
VBM(E ⊗F, R × M) ∼= VBM(F, E∗),
∀F ∈VBM,
(1)
where VBM is the category of vector bundles over M with bundle maps as mor-
phisms. This property can be stated as the fact that E∗classifies the space of
vector bundle pairings with E. The dual itself has a canonical evaluation pairing
⟨·, ·⟩: E∗⊗E −→R × M,
which corresponds to the identity under the isomorphism of hom spaces above.
In the specific example of the tangent bundle of a manifold TM →M, this
construction returns the cotangent bundle T∗M. The canonical pairing induces
the tautological 1-form θ ∈Ω1(T∗M), which is defined by
θξ(V) := ⟨ξ, TqV⟩,
∀ξ ∈T∗M, V ∈TξT∗M,
(2)
where q : T∗M →M is the bundle projection. The de Rham differential of θ
defines the canonical symplectic form ωcan := −dθ on T∗M. This means it is a
closed 2-form and it defines a nondegenerate antisymmetric pairing
ωcan : TT∗M ∧TT∗M −→R × T∗M.
The nondegeneracy condition is equivalent to the fact that the associated map
ω♭
can : TT∗M →T∗T∗M induced by applying the adjunction (1) is an isomor-
phism. This structure is of fundamental importance in the theory of hamilto-
nian mechanics and is often considered to be the most fundamental example of
symplectic geometry: by the Darboux theorem, any k-dimensional symplectic
manifold (M, ω) is locally isomorphic to (T∗Rk, ωcan).
A Lie groupoid is a generalization of a smooth manifold (see e.g. [CW00;
Mac05]). This is a (small) category where the set of object and the set of arrows
1We write this as a bundle map with target R × M to emphasize the fact that it is defined
fiberwise, even though, after evaluating, the basepoint is usually omitted.


## Page 6


ii
Preface
are smooth manifolds, all arrows are invertible, and where the source and target
maps are surjective submersions. This is commonly pictured as the diagram
G1 ⇒G0,
where G1 is the manifold of arrows, G0 is the manifold of points, and the hori-
zontal arrows are the source and target maps, which we denote by d0 and d1, re-
spectively. Some examples of Lie groupoids are Lie groups, which have G0 = pt,
manifolds, which have G1 = G0, action groupoids G × M ⇒M, where M is
a manifold acted on by a Lie group G, principal bundles, and vector bundles,
among others.
A vector bundle over a Lie groupoid is known as a VB-groupoid (e.g. [Mac05;
GM17]). This is a Lie groupoid in the category of vector bundles. It can be
pictured as a diagram
V1
⇒
V0
↓
↓
G1
⇒
G0,
where the vertical maps are vector bundle projections and Lie groupoid mor-
phisms, and the horizontal maps are vector bundle maps. The multiplication
of the total space V1 is also a vector bundle map over the multiplication in G1,
leading to the so-called interchange law,
(wh · vg) + (w′
h · v′
g) = (wh + w′
h) · (vg + v′
g),
for any pairs of composable arrows (vg, wh) and (v′
g, w′

of composable arrows (g, h) in the base G1.
A duality for VB-groupoids was identified by Pradines in [Pra88] (see also
[Mac05]). To rephrase it in similar terms as we did for vector bundles we im-
mediately run into the problem that the tensor product of VB-groupoids is not
generally a VB-groupoid itself.2 This is a natural point to introduce higher Lie
groupoids and higher vector bundles over them, in order to consider tensor prod-
ucts of VB-groupoids.
We follow the simplicial point of view that started with Duskin’s hypergroup-
oids (see [Dus79; Gle82; Dus01]) and was extended to the smooth setting in
[Get09; Hen08; Zhu09] and studied in the later works [Pri13; Li14; Wol16; BG17;
RZ20], among others.
By applying the nerve construction to a Lie groupoid, (see example 1.1.9 for
details) this can be seen as a simplicial manifold satisfying certain Kan condi-
tions:
• Kan(1, j), for j = 0, 1: the source and target map are surjective submer-
sions,
• Kan!(m, j), for j = 0, . . . , m and any m > 1: any sequence of m arrows
forming a zig-zag identifies a unique m-simplex.
2Pradines uses the levelwise Whitney sum of V and its dual as the domain of the canonical
pairing and requires that it is a bilinear VB-groupoid morphism, to avoid precisely this problem.
With this formulation the universal property interpretation in terms of a tensor-hom adjunction
is lost.


## Page 7


Preface
iii
By abstracting these conditions one observes that the difference between them
is that certain projections are surjective submersions in the case of the Kan con-
dition Kan(m, j), and diffeomorphisms in the case of the strict Kan condition
Kan!(m, j). A Lie n-groupoid can be defined as a simplicial manifold with all
Kan conditions for m ≥1, 0 ≤j ≤m and strict Kan conditions for m > n,
0 ≤j ≤m, as we write in Definition 1.2.16. If only the non-strict Kan conditions
are required to hold, this defines a Lie ∞-groupoid. When forgetting the smooth
structure, these are Kan complexes (see e.g. [GJ09]), though requiring a simpli-
cial manifold to be a set-theoretical Kan complex is not enough to obtain a Lie
∞-groupoid, as discussed in detail in [RZ20].
In this picture, smooth manifolds are Lie 0-groupoids, and Lie groupoids are
Lie 1-groupoids by identifying them with their nerves. Lie groupoid morphisms
are then simplicial maps between the nerves. It is worth noting that any simpli-
cial map with target a Lie n-groupoid is determined by its first n + 1 levels, i.e. its
action on the 0 to (n + 1)-simplices of the source, as we discuss in Section 1.2.5.
Following this line of thought, a VB n-groupoid is a simplicial vector bundle
over a Lie n-groupoid which is itself a Lie n-groupoid, as we write in Definition
1.4.2. These were defined in [HT24]. More concretely, each level is a vector bun-
dle, the face and degeneracy maps are bundle maps over the corresponding maps
of the base, and the bundle projections commute with the face and degeneracy
maps. A higher vector bundle over a Lie n-groupoid G is a VB ∞-groupoid over
G. In this category, the tensor product of two higher vector bundles is simply the
levelwise tensor product of vector bundles applied as a functor to all the face and
degeneracy maps, as well. Then, even if the tensor product of two VB-groupoids
is not a VB-groupoid, it is still a higher vector bundle of generally higher order.
We discuss this in more detail in Remark 3.1.4.
Going back to VB-groupoids, we can now phrase their duality in terms of 1-
shifted simplicial pairings, as defined in Definition 3.1.5. Consider the additive
group B1R = (R ⇒0) which is topologically the Eilenberg-Maclane space
K(R, 1). Given a VB-groupoid V →G, Pradines’s dual V1∗→G is uniquely
determined3 by the property that the canonical evaluation pairing
ev : V∗
1 ⊗V1 −→R × G1
is normalized and multiplicative, i.e. it extends to a 1-shifted simplicial VB-
pairing

The first immediate example of a VB-groupoid is the tangent groupoid TG of
a Lie groupoid G.

This is obtained by simply applying the tangent functor to the spaces and maps
defining G. By dualizing the tangent groupoid TG of a Lie groupoid G with
the aforementioned procedure, one obtains the cotangent groupoid T1∗G. The
canonical 1-shifted simplicial pairing ⟨·, ·⟩defines again a 1-shifted tautological
form θ as in (2), which, when differentiated, gives the canonical symplectic form
ωcan. This form is closed, because it is exact, but it is also multiplicative, i.e. it
induces a 1-shifted simplicial (antisymmetric) pairing
ωcan : TT1∗G ∧TT1∗G −→B1R × T1∗G,
which is equivalent to the fact that ωcan ∈Ω2(T1∗G1) is a closed form in the
total complex of the Bott-Shulman-Stasheff double complex from [BSS76], see
also [AC13; Get; CZ23]. Finally ωcan is nondegenerate in the usual sense, so
(T1∗G, ωcan) is a symplectic groupoid, as shown in [CDW87].
So far, we only re-elaborated pre-existing results. In this thesis, we unify and
generalize the duality theory of vector bundles and VB-groupoids to one where
the canonical pairing is n-shifted. We define the n-dual Vn∗of a higher vector
bundle V over a Lie n-groupoid G as the universal object classifying n-shifted
simplicial pairings with V. That is, Vn∗represents the functor

When applying this duality to the tangent groupoid of a Lie n-groupoid one
obtains the n-cotangent. Under the correspondence between Morita classes of n-
groupoids and n-stacks, which was studied in different settings in [Pri13; BG17;
RZ20], the n-cotangent of a Lie n-groupoid should correspond to the cotangent
stack studied in [Cal19]. This correspondence is not clear to us at the moment,
but we plan to study it in future work.
About the problem of existence of the n-dual, we find that the existence of the
n-dual for a given higher vector bundle is subject to existence and regularity of
the solutions of certain equations. I.e. the n-dual can be expressed levelwise as a
limit in VB, and the structure of the equations shows that it is entirely determined
by solving these only for levels 0 to n + 1. It is however not clear a priori if this
is automatically a VB n-groupoid. This is discussed in Section 3.2.

Theorem 1. The n-dual of a higher vector bundle is given levelwise by a limit in VB,
and it is determined by its levels 0 to n + 1.
Consider for the moment the case of a VB n-groupoid V →G. Assuming that
the n-dual of V exists, we find that the canonical evaluation pairing is nondege-
nerate in the homology of the higher vector bundle. The homology of a higher
vector bundle is discussed after Definition 1.4.14, and the homological nonde-
generacy condition in Definition 3.1.10. This is the same nondegeneracy condi-
tion that n-shifted symplectic structures are required to satisfy in [Get], where this
notion was translated to the smooth setting from the work of [PTVV13]. This is
the content of Theorem 3.2.15, which consists of a slightly more general statement
than the one reported here.

dual Vn∗exists. Then the n-dual pairing ⟨·, ·⟩: Vn∗
n ⊗Vn →R × Gn is homologically
n-shifted nondegenerate.
At this point we focus on the n = 2 case and provide a concrete model of the
VB 2-dual of a VB 2-groupoid. This is one of the main accomplishments of this
thesis. We show the following properties in Section 3.3.
Theorem 3. Let V →G be a VB 2-groupoid. Its VB 2-dual V2∗exists and it is a VB
2-groupoid.
Theorem 4. A 2-shifted simplicial pairing α : V ⊗W →B2R × G between two VB
2-groupoids V and W over the same base G is homologically nondegenerate if and only
if it induces weak equivalences
αl : W →V2∗,
αr : V →W2∗.
This provides as a characterization of the nondegeneracy of 2-shifted sym-
plectic structures over Lie 2-groupoids. This result is implied by the following
one, which we prove in Theorem 1.4.21.
Theorem 5. A simplicial bundle map f : V →W between two VB 2-groupoids over G
over the identity of G is a weak equivalence if and only if it induces a quasi-isomorphism
between their normalized complexes.
This is an extension to n = 2 of [HO20, Thm. 3.5] in the case of a map over
the identity on the base.
In the last chapter, we consider the 2-cotangent of a Lie 2-groupoid, that is the
2-dual of its tangent groupoid. We show that the definition of the tautological
form given in (2) goes through and we obtain a canonical 2-shifted symplectic
structure in Theorem 4.1.7.
Theorem 6. Let G be a Lie 2-groupoid, and T2∗G its 2-cotangent. The canonical form
ω := −dθ, where θ ∈Ω1(T2∗G2) is given as in (2) with respect to the 2-dual pairing,
is a 2-shifted symplectic form.
For the 2-cotangent of a Lie 1-groupoid G, we find a symplectic morita equiv-
alence between T2∗G and Ę
T1∗G, that is, the 2-shifted symplectic VB 2-groupoid
obtained by applying the Artin-Mazur bar to the 1-cotangent T1∗G.

Theorem 7. Let G be a Lie groupoid and (κ, σ) an Ehresmann connection on G. Then,
the map
Ξ : T2∗G →T1∗G
defined by (4.24) is a symplectic Morita equivalence.
The VB 2-groupoid Ę
T1∗G appeared in [MT11] as the 2-groupoid integrating
the Courant algebroid A ⊕A∗, that is the double of the Lie algebroid A of G
(See [LWX97]). So Theorem 7 frames T2∗G as an integration up to homotopy of the
Courant algebroid A ⊕A∗. We discuss this point of view further in Chapter 4.
It is worth noting that the explicit Morita equivalence we are able to construct
depends on a choice of Ehresmann connection on the groupoid, while both T2∗G
and Ę
T1∗G are independent of choices.
In proving many of these results we use techniques that we import from the
simpler case of simplicial vector spaces, which are higher vector bundles over
the point.4 The link that allows us to import these methods from the theory of
simplicial vector spaces to that of higher vector bundles over G lies in the fact
that, by using the unique embedding of G0 into any other level of G given by the
total degeneracy
1 = (s0)k : G0 →Gk,
a higher vector bundle V over G can be pulled back to a higher vector bundle
1∗V over the 0-groupoid G0. This is a “bundle of simplicial vector spaces”: its
simplicial structure is defined pointwise over G0.
The VB duality theory for n > 1 is much more complex than for n = 0 and
n = 1, starting from the fact that existence of n-duals is, a priori, unclear. Many
of these complexities already manifest themselves in the case of simplicial vector
spaces. However, this particular case is simpler, as existence of n-duals is guar-
anteed for all simplicial vector spaces by the existence of a right adjoint to the
tensor product: the mapping space functor, see e.g. [GJ09]. Thus, the universal
property defining the n-dual over the point is satisfied by the mapping space
Hom(V, BnR). As we show in Theorem 1.2.10, this is an n-groupoid. From now
on, we call n-groupoids in the category of vector spaces VS n-groupoids.
The case of simplicial vector spaces gives a clear picture of the structure of
n-duals, a model of which can be obtained by solving the equations computing
Hom(V, BnR). We discuss the structure of these equations in Section 2.3 and we
solve them explicitly in order to compute n-duals of VS n-groupoids for n = 1, 2.
For n = 1 we recover the VB 1-dual of [Pra88] and for n = 2 we describe a new
object, which we call the 2-dual.
The aforementioned homology theory of simplicial vector spaces comes from
the Dold-Kan correspondence (see e.g. [Wei94; GJ09]), which gives an equiva-
lence of categories

The homology of V is defined as the homology of N(V).
Additionally, the Dold-Kan correspondence is an equivalence between the ho-
motopy categories, and in particular, a simplicial linear map f : V →W is a
weak equivalence, if and only if N( f ) : N(V) →N(W) is a quasi-isomorphism.
In other words, all the information of a VS n-groupoid, including its homotopy
type, is encoded in its normalized complex. Thus, if the homology of V vanishes
above degree n, we say that V is a VS n-type. Naturally any VS n-groupoid is an
n-type.
There is a problem though, in that the Dold-Kan correspondence is not mo-
noidal on the nose, as it does not preserve the tensor product on each side, but it
does so only up to homotopy equivalence. This is the content of the Eilenberg-
Zilber theorem, in its original version appearing in [EZ53; EM54]:
Let V and W be two simplicial vector spaces. There is a natural chain ho-
motopy equivalence given by the Eilenberg-Zilber map EZ and the Alexander-
Whitney map AW,
N(V) ⊗N(W)
EZ
−−→
←−−
AW N(V ⊗W),
such that
AW ◦EZ = idN(V)⊗N(W),
EZ ◦AW ∼idN(V⊗W).
With this homotopy equivalence, we can translate an n-shifted pairing of two
simplicial vector spaces into an n-shifted IM-pairing5 between the normalized
complexes:
α : V ⊗W →BnR
⇝
λα = EZ∗◦N(α) : N(V) ⊗N(W) →R[−n].
This is the same operation used to obtain the IM-pairing on the tangent complex
associated to an n-shifted symplectic form in [Get]. This allows us to define the
homological nondegeneracy condition for any pairing of simplicial vector spaces,
as in Definition 2.2.12.
Of course since the Eilenberg-Zilber theorem affects the tensor product, it also
affects its adjoint, the mapping space. In one of our main results (Theorem 2.1.4),
we make this precise, by reformulating the theorem in terms of mapping spaces.

Because of the truncation that happens at 0 in the right side, if N(V)∗[−n] has
non-zero negative degrees, there is some loss of information when taking the
n-dual. When V is a VS n-groupoid, its normalized complex is concentrated in
degrees 0 to n, which means that N(V)∗[−n] is concentrated in degrees 0 to n
as well, and it coincides with its zero truncation. Even when V is not a VS n-
groupoid on the nose but it is homotopy equivalent to one (i.e. it is an n-type),
there is no loss of homotopy information, as its homology is still concentrated in
degrees 0 to n. This is expressed by Theorem 2.2.13.
Theorem 9. Let V be a simplicial vector space. The n-dual pairing ⟨·, ·⟩: Vn∗⊗V →
BnR is homologically n-shifted nondegenerate if and only if V is at most an n-type.
We observe that this is the right nondegeneracy condition, since the canonical
2-dual pairing is degenerate on the nose, but nondegenerate in homology.
As an immediate consequence, we characterize nondegeneracy of pairings in
terms of homotopy instead of homology (Theorem 2.2.17).
Theorem 10. An n-shifted simplicial pairing α between VS n-types V and W is homo-
logically nondegenerate if and only if it induces a weak equivalence
αr : V →Wn∗,
or equivalently, a weak equivalence
αl : W →Vn∗.
A main application of the above result is that n-duality is reflexive up to ho-
motopy (Theorem 2.2.20).
Corollary 11. Let V be at most an n-type. The double n-dual (Vn∗)n∗is weak equivalent
to V itself. That is

Notation and conventions
We use the homological convention, in which the Dold-Kan correspondence is an
equivalence between simplicial vector spaces and non-negative chain complexes.
We orient edges of simplices from higher to lower number, “as the water flows”,
thus the source of an arrow is its d0 face, while its target is its d1 face. We also
write boundaries and horns as tuples ordered by increasing face index. This
has the effect that our version of the nerve of a category (Example 1.1.9) might
appear to be flipped by the front-to-back symmetry (Remark 1.1.11) with respect
to other perhaps more common conventions. We sometimes use the terms weak
equivalence and Morita equivalence interchangeably, when talking about weak
equivalences of higher groupoids. Other conventions will be explained as they
appear.

Chapter 1
Background Notions
In this chapter, we discuss the foundations of the theory of higher groupoids,
higher vector spaces and higher vector bundles. Most of the material is a re-
elaboration of more or less established literature which we reference as it occurs,
except for a couple of original results which we highlight as they arise. For the
sake of brevity we omit most specific references from this summary, as they are
provided in the corresponding sections.
Throughout the chapter, we mostly consider two kinds of categories: cate-
gories such as Set and Vec which have all categorical limits and colimits (i.e. they
are complete and cocomplete), and categories such as those of smooth manifolds
Mfd and vector bundles VB, which are not complete nor cocomplete. Naturally,
the theory for the second kind of category is complicated by the (a priori) ab-
sence of certain limits, and we review a series of techniques to address this.
In Section 1.1 we begin with a basic discussion of simplicial objects in a general
category. We focus on the combinatorial structure of the standard simplex, which
is necessary for computing n-duals. We then review some key structures on the
category of simplicial sets: the monoidal structure given by the levelwise prod-
uct, and the mapping set (or function complex), which is a simplicial set with
simplicial maps as points, simplicial homotopies between maps as 1-simplices
and higher homotopies as higher simplices. These two functors form a tensor-
hom adjunction, making the category of simplicial sets a category enriched over
itself. We frame this in terms of a general method to construct categories enriched
over simplicial sets, which we later apply to simplicial vector spaces.
In the lengthy Section 1.2 we introduce the simplicial model of higher group-
oids, beginning with n-groupoids in the category of simplicial sets. A funda-
mental idea in this theory is the concept of an object of simplicial diagrams, which
collects arrangements of simplices of a certain shape within a simplicial object.
In the first subsection we consider the setting of simplicial sets, and define n-
groupoids by imposing the Kan conditions. We then recall that Kan simplicial
sets (∞-groupoids) admit homotopy groups. These homotopy invariants lead
to the notion of weak homotopy equivalences, which allow one to compare dif-
ferent n-groupoids up to homotopy. In this context, an n-groupoid represents a
homotopy n-type, i.e. a space with trivial homotopy groups for m > n. We con-
clude the discussion of n-groupoids in Set by recalling the fact that any mapping
space with target a Kan simplicial set is also Kan and upgrading this statement


## Page 20


2
Background Notions
(by assembling various results in the literature) to the fact that any mapping
space with n-groupoid target is an n-groupoid. This is the content of Theorem
1.2.10.
In the second subsection, we discuss objects of simplicial diagrams for sim-
plicial manifolds. As these are obtained by computing categorical limits, they
might not exist as manifolds, but we show how they can be studied by consider-
ing them first as sheaves on Mfd with the pretopology of surjective submersions.
With this, we state the Kan conditions that define Lie n-groupoids.
In the third subsection we recall a framework to study representability of ob-
jects of simplicial diagrams and how this can be used to show that the horn spaces
appearing in the Kan conditions are representable. We then use this to produce
a new result computing the dimension of the horn spaces by writing them as
fiber products over surjective submersions. This is Theorem 1.2.34 and Corol-
lary 1.2.35.
In the fourth subsection we discuss weak equivalences of Lie n-groupoids
from the perspective of n-groupoids in a general category with a pretopology
and a jointly conservative collection of point functors. This is a device that al-
lows one to show two objects are isomorphic if and only if there is a bijection
between them after applying each point functor. This is useful in concrete cate-
gories where the forgetful functor does not reflect isomorphisms, such as in Mfd.
We employ this to define weak equivalences stalkwise, i.e. two objects are weakly
equivalent if and only if they are so in SSet after applying each functor of points.
We then describe a more practical characterization of weak equivalences which
recovers the notion of Morita maps and Morita equivalences present in the liter-
ature on Lie groupoids.
In the final subsection, we discuss how to recover a Lie n-groupoid from a
finite set of data, interpreting it as a space with certain multiplication and inver-
sion maps. To do this on a general category with a pretopology we introduce the
coskeleton of a simplicial object, which is also a levelwise limit (so a priori not
representable), and use a collection of points to prove results about it.
In Section 1.3 we study simplicial vector spaces in more detail and review the
theorem of Moore which states that they are automatically Kan complexes by
virtue of being simplicial groups. In preparation to discuss the classical Dold-
Kan correspondence between simplicial vector spaces SVec and non-negative
chain complexes Ch≥0(Vec), we recall the structures of SVec, Ch(Vec), and its
subcategory Ch≥0(Vec) as monoidal categories enriched over themselves. This
gives mapping spaces for each of these categories. By considering maps up to ho-
motopy, each of these categories has a homotopy category. We then recall that the
Dold-Kan correspondence is not only an equivalence of categories through the
normalized complex functor, but it extends to an equivalence of the homotopy
categories. As such, weak equivalences of simplicial vector spaces are the same as
quasi-isomorphisms between the respective normalized complexes, and homo-
topy groups of a simplicial vector space are the same as the homology groups of
its normalized complexes. As such VS n-groupoids can be seen as linear n-types.
We conclude this section by showing that VS n-groupoids have a more restric-
tive finite data than general n-groupoids, in the sense that the Moore theorem
prescribes canonical multiplication maps for each VS n-groupoid. This is com-


## Page 21


1.1 Simplicial objects
3
patible with the fact that a simplicial vector space is an n-groupoid if and only
if its normalized complex is concentrated in degrees 0 to n, as a consequence of
the Dold-Kan correspondence.
In Section 1.4 we close the chapter by introducing higher vector bundles over
Lie n-groupoids. These are simplicial objects in the category of vector bundles
which are themselves Lie n-groupoids for some n (possibly ∞). Their theory
is simplified by the fact that surjective submersions of vector bundles are the
same as fiberwise linear maps over surjective submersions that are also fiber-
wise surjective. The important fact which links them to the theory of simplicial
vector spaces is that any higher vector bundle over the Lie n-groupoid G can be
pulled back by the total degeneracy map to a higher vector bundle over the 0-
groupoid G0. This can then be seen as a “bundle of simplicial vector spaces”, as
its simplicial structure is defined entirely pointwise over G0. After recalling the
construction of the dual VB 1-groupoid and the cotangent of a Lie 1-groupoid,
we define the normalized complex of a simplicial vector bundle, as the fiber-
wise normalized complex of the pullback by the total units. This allows us to
consider VB n-groupoids as pointwise n-types and will allow us to prove many
results in the VB case by using the same theory as for simplicial vector spaces
applied pointwise. We conclude the section by taking this correspondence fur-
ther and showing our main result of this chapter. This is a partial extension of a
theorem relating weak equivalences of VB 1-groupoids and quasi-isomorphisms
of their normalized complexes from [HO20], to one relating weak equivalences
of VB 2-groupoids (over the identity on the base) to quasi-isomorphisms of their
normalized complexes. This is the content of Theorem 1.4.21.
1.1
Simplicial objects
A groupoid of local transformations is commonly pictured as a set of 1-dimen-
sional oriented edges (arrows) between points in a set. Higher categorical infor-
mation comes in the form of transformations between transformations, which ge-
ometrically translates to the existence of non-trivial higher-dimensional cells. In
the simplicial formalism, higher-dimensional cells take the shape of n-simplices.
The geometry of simplices is encoded in the combinatorics of the category ∆of
isomorphism classes of finite ordered sets
[0] := {0},
[1] := {0 ≤1},
. . . ,
[n] := {0 ≤1 ≤· · · ≤n},
. . .
and weakly order-preserving maps between them. This leads to the theory of
simplicial sets and simplicial objects.
Standard references on the subject are
[May67; GJ09]. For a pedagogical introduction to simplicial sets see [Fri12].
Definition 1.1.1. A simplicial object in a category C is a functor ∆op →C.
The category of simplicial objects and simplicial maps in C is denoted by SC =
Fun(∆op, C).
A simplicial set is a functor ∆op →Set. We call the natural transformations
between such functors simplicial maps. The category of simplicial sets and sim-
plicial maps is denoted by SSet := Fun(∆op, Set).


## Page 22


4
Background Notions
Analogously, simplicial vector spaces, simplicial manifolds and simplicial
vector bundles are simplicial objects in the categories Vec of vector spaces with
linear maps, Mfd of smooth manifolds with smooth maps, and VB of smooth
vector bundles with smooth bundle maps, respectively.
Definition 1.1.2. A concrete category C is a category equipped with a faithful
functor U : C →Set called the forgetful functor which returns the so-called
underlying set of each object.
When the meaning is clear from context, we omit U and denote the underlying
set of an object by the same name as that object.
Remark 1.1.3. The categories Set, Vec, Mfd, and VB are concrete categories in
which the forgetful functor is representable, and therefore preserves limits. (See
e.g. [Rie16, Thm 3.4.6]). In fact, when C is Set or Mfd, then U is represented by
the point pt. The forgetful functor for vector spaces Vec is Vec(R, _), while that
for vector bundles with bundle maps VB is VB(R →pt, _).
Remark 1.1.4. The forgetful functor of a concrete category extends immediately
to a forgetful functor U : SC →SSet, by postcomposition of every simplicial
object in C with the forgetful functor of C. We call this the underlying simplicial
set of a simplicial object.
Example 1.1.5. The most immediate examples of simplicial sets are given by the
hom-functors of the category ∆. We call these standard simplices and denote
them by
∆n := ∆(_, [n]).
The structure of simplicial objects is made more concrete by the fact that the
category ∆is generated by just two classes of maps.

Remark 1.1.11 (Front-to-back duality [Wei94, §8.2.10][Lur24, Tag 003L]). Every
simplicial object X in a category C has an opposite X op, which consists of the
same spaces but has simplicial maps (dop)n
i = dn
n−i, (sop)n
i = sn
n−i. This can be
understood as reversing the order of the elements in each object of the category
∆, or equivalently reversing the direction of all the edges and orienting them
from lower to higher vertex number. When the simplicial object in question is
the nerve of a category, its opposite is precisely the nerve of the opposite cate-
gory [Lur24, Tag 003Q]. The existence of opposite simplicial objects extends the
well-known principle of categorical duality (see e.g. [Rie16, §1.2]) to this con-
text. In particular, if a property holds for any simplicial object under hypotheses
symmetric with respect to the opposite construction, it must hold for its opposite
as well, which can be helpful in certain proofs. We will make use of this property
in the computation of 1- and 2-duals in Section 2.3.

When writing a simplex in ∆n as u = u0 . . . um we are listing its vertices. If
any of these are repeated, the simplex u is degenerate and this represents the fact
that a lower dimensional simplex can be seen as a collapsed or “very thin” higher
dimensional simplex. On the other hand, taking diu means considering the face
of u opposite the vertex ui.

Definition 1.1.15 ([May67, Def. I.6.5]). For any integers p, q ≥0, a (p, q)-shuffle
is a permutation π ∈Sp+q such that
π(i) < π(j),
if 0 ≤i < j ≤p −1 or if p ≤i < j ≤p + q −1.
By writing µi = π(i −1) for any 1 ≤i ≤p and νi = π(p + i −1) for any
1 ≤i ≤q, each shuffle π is identified with a pair of increasing multi-indices
(µ, ν), where one item of the pair determines the other. That is
π = (µ, ν) = (µ1 . . . µp, ν1 . . . νq) = (π(0) . . . π(p −1), π(p) . . . π(p + q −1)).
The sign of a shuffle sgn(µ, ν) is defined as the sign of the corresponding permu-
tation π.
The set of (p, q)-shuffles is denoted by Sh(p, q), and it has cardinality (p+q
p ).
As a convention, we define Sh(p, q) := ∅for any negative integer p or q.
Example 1.1.16. Some sets of shuffles are
∀n ≥1, Sh(n, 0) = {id = (µ = 0 . . . n)} ∼= Sh(0, n) = {id = (ν = 0 . . . n)};
Sh(1, 1) = {id = (0, 1), (1, 0)},

Sh(1, 2) = {id = (0, 12), (1, 02), (2, 01)},

Sh(2, 2) = {id = (01, 23), (02, 13), (03, 12), (12, 03), (13, 02), (23, 01)}, . . .

Remark 1.1.18. Because strictly increasing multi-indices such as those in Remark
1.1.10 identify shuffles, this can be used to classify all possible compositions of
face and degeneracy maps between any two levels of a simplicial object X by
using the normal form (1.6). In particular, for any m ≥1 there is a unique such
map 1 : X0 →Xm, which is
1 = sm−1 ◦· · · ◦s1 ◦s0,

and it coincides with any other possible composition of degeneracy maps from
X0 to Xm. We call this map the total unit. Its associated multi-index corresponds
to the unique (m, 0)-shuffle given by the identity of {0, . . . , m −1}.
1.1.2
Products and mapping sets
We now describe products and mapping spaces of simplicial sets. The material
in this section is fundamental in simplicial homotopy theory and can be found
for example in [May67; GJ09; Fri12; Rie14; Lur24; Stacks].
To begin with, we define the product of two simplicial objects in any category
with binary products. When applied to simplicial sets, this gives a symmetric
monoidal structure on SSet.


## Page 29


1.1 Simplicial objects
11
Definition 1.1.19. Let X and Y be simplicial objects in C. The product X × Y is
the simplicial set defined at any level n ≥0 by
(X × Y)n := Xn × Yn,
with simplicial maps
di(x, y) = (dix, diy),
si(x, y) = (six, siy),
for all (x, y) ∈(X × Y)n and any appropriate i.
Definition 1.1.20. Let C be a category with all (finite) coproducts of an object
with itself. Let S be a (levelwise finite) simplicial set and X a simplicial object
in C. The copowering X ⊗S is the simplicial set defined at each level n ≥0 by
(X ⊗S)n := ⨿
u∈Sn
X u
n ,
(1.17)
with simplicial maps defined by
di(xu) = (dix)diu,
si(xu) = (six)siu,
for all xu ∈(X ⊗S)n and any appropriate i.
In the situation of the above definition we say that SC is copowered over (lev-
elwise finite) simplicial sets. The categories of levelwise finite-dimensional SVec
and SMfd are copowered over levelwise finite simplicial sets.
Remark 1.1.21. It is clear that if C = Set, the product and the copowering of two
simplicial sets with each other coincide.
The following lemma is an immediate consequence of the definitions above
and it can be proven by constructing the obvious natural isomorphisms. It is part
of [GJ09, Thm. II.2.5].
Lemma 1.1.22 (Associativity of the copowering). Let X ∈SC, and S, T be level-
wise finite simplicial sets. Then
(X ⊗S) ⊗T ∼= X ⊗(S × T ).
Definition 1.1.23. Let X , Y be simplicial objects in C and f, g : X →Y two
simplicial maps between them. A simplicial homotopy h between f and g is a
simplicial map
h : X ⊗∆1 →Y
such that
d0h = h ◦(id ⊗δ0) = f : X ⊗∆0 ∼= X →Y, and
d1h = h ◦(id ⊗δ1) = g : X ⊗∆0 ∼= X →Y.
A similar definition can be used to recursively define higher homotopies be-
tween homotopies.

and the obvious identity element, SC is an SSet-category, in the sense of [Rie14,
Def. 3.3.1]. Categories enriched over simplicial sets are also known as simplicial
categories, see e.g. [Rie14, §3.6], [Lur24, Tag 00JQ].
If C is the category of sets, we denote the mapping set by Maps. This is pre-
cisely the internal hom of simplicial sets with respect to the monoidal structure
given by the product. We report this adjunction in the following proposition.
This makes SSet a cartesian closed category: it is closed monoidal with respect
to the cartesian product.


## Page 31


1.1 Simplicial objects
13
Proposition 1.1.26 (Enriched tensor-hom adjunction). Let X , Y and Z be simpli-
cial sets. Then there are natural isomorphisms
Maps(X × Y, Z) ∼= Maps(X , Maps(Y, Z)) ∼= Maps(Y, Maps(X , Z)).
Proof. This proof is an extension of [GJ09, Prop. I.5.1]. We show a version of this
result explicitly for simplicial vector spaces in Proposition 1.3.6, since we will use
it in Chapter 2.
Remark 1.1.27. As mentioned above, we are using the term “simplicial category”
to indicate a simplicially enriched category or an SSet category, following [Rie14;
Lur24]. There are other notions of simplicial category in the literature, such as
the one in [GJ09, Def. II.2.1]. In this definition, for SC to be a simplicial category,
in addition to the simplicial enrichment, they require that:
1. The mapping set functor has a left adjoint, called the tensoring or copow-
ering functor
_ ⊗_ : SC × SSet →SC
with the associativity property in Lemma 1.1.22,
2. The mapping set functor has a right adjoint, called the cotensoring or pow-
ering functor
hom(_, _) : SSetop × SC →SC.
In other words, these three functors must fit into a two-variable adjunction
SC(X ⊗S, Y) ∼= SSet(S, Hom(X , Y)) ∼= SC(Y, hom(S, Y)),
(1.20)
for any X , Y ∈SC and S ∈SSet.
A simplicially enriched category where (1) holds is said to be copowered over
simplicial sets. In [Rie14, Def. 3.7.2] the term tensored is used instead. On the
other hand, a simplicially enriched category where (2) holds is said to be pow-
ered over simplicial sets, or analogously cotensored, as in [Rie14, Def. 3.7.3].
See also [Shu06, §14].
Remark 1.1.28. The category of simplicial sets satisfies all the properties in Re-
mark 1.1.27, and the powering functor coincides with the mapping set functor.
In fact, Proposition 1.1.26 is a SSet-enriched version of adjunction (1.20), which
holds for all enriched, copowered and powered categories over simplicial sets
and is reported without proof in [GJ09, Lemma II.2.3] and [Rie14, Rem. 3.7.4].
As we will see in Section 1.3.1, the category of simplicial vector spaces is en-
riched, copowered and powered over SSet but also over itself, and an analogous
SVec-enriched adjunction holds.
Example 1.1.29. Any category with self products and coproducts is powered and
copowered over sets, respectively. We use this implicitly in Definition 1.1.20,
where (1.17) describes level n of X ⊗S as the copowering over sets of the level
n of X over the respective level of S.

Background Notions
1.2
Higher Groupoids
The definition of higher groupoids in terms of simplicial sets or simplicial objects
in a more general topos was first given in [Dus79; Gle82]. This was then adapted
to higher Lie groupoids in the work of [Get09; Hen08; Zhu09].
We begin by discussing the definitions in the category of simplicial sets. All of
these constructions generalize without any problem to higher groupoid objects
in Vec, as we recall in Section 1.3. For higher Lie groupoids more care is needed
and this is the bulk of the discussion in the later subsections.
1.2.1
Simplicial diagrams and groupoids
The correspondence between n-simplices in a simplicial object X and simplicial
maps ∆n →X extends to simplicial diagrams in X of a certain shape given by a
simplicial set S and simplicial maps S →X . This is analogous to the definition
of a diagram in a category C as a functor J →C with J a small indexing category
representing the shape of the diagram.
Definition 1.2.1. Let S be a levelwise finite simplicial set, and X a simplicial
set. A simplicial diagram in X of shape S is a simplicial map S →X . The
set of simplicial diagrams of shape S in X is SSet(S, X ) and we denote it by
hom(S, X ).
Remark 1.2.2. The notation hom(S, X ) comes from the fact that this is level 0 of
the powering of simplicial sets with itself, which is defined by hom(S, X )m =
hom(S ⊗∆m, X ). That is, higher levels of the powering functor are diagrams in
the shape of prisms with base S inside the second argument. This works for sim-
plicial vector spaces too: as we see in Section 1.3, the space of simplicial diagrams
in a simplicial vector space can be used to define the powering of simplicial vector
spaces over simplicial sets.
The most important shapes of simplicial diagrams we will consider are horns
and boundaries.
Definition 1.2.3. For any integer m ≥0 the m-boundary ∂∆m is the simplicial
subset of the m-simplex ∆m obtained by removing the m-simplex Em and all of
its degenerate simplices. In other words, at each level l ≥0,
(∂∆m)l = { f ∈(∆m)l | Im f ⊉{0, . . . , m}} ⊆∆m
l .
In particular ∂∆0 = ∅.
For any integers m ≥0 and 0 ≤k ≤m, the (m, k)-horn Λm
k is the simplicial
subset of the m-boundary ∂∆m obtained by removing the k-th face dkEn and all
its degenerate simplices. In other words, at each level l ≥0,
(Λm
k )l = { f ∈(∆m)l | Im f ⊉{0, . . . ,pk, . . . , m}} ⊆(∂∆m)l ⊆∆m
l .
Again, clearly Λ0
0 = ∅.

A central concept in simplicial methods and homotopy theory is that of a lift-
ing problem. (See e.g. [GJ09]). In one of its forms, this is the question of whether
for a map of simplicial sets i : S →T and a simplicial map f : X →Y, any dia-

Background Notions
To understand this, we start by considering the case where Y is ∆0 = pt, the
terminal object of SSet and f : X →pt is the canonical map sending everything
to the point. We will discuss the general case in Section 1.2.4.
When Y is the point, the existence of a lift — or more properly an extension,
if i is an inclusion — of any map S →X to a map T →X is equivalent to the
fact that the precomposition
i∗: hom(T , X ) →hom(S, X )
is surjective. Moreover, the uniqueness of this lift is equivalent to i∗being injec-
tive. In terms of simplicial diagrams this can be translated as
“Any simplicial diagram of shape S in X determines a (unique)
simplicial diagram of shape T in X .”
The lifting problems with respect to the horn inclusions Λm
k ,→∆m lead to the
Kan conditions and the definition of higher groupoids. Solutions of such lifting
problems are called horn fillers. See Figure 1.3 for a pictorial representation of
these.
Definition 1.2.4. Let n be a non-negative integer or ∞. An n-groupoid X is a
simplicial set satisfying the Kan conditions Kan(m, k) for any m ≥1, 0 ≤k ≤m
and the strict Kan conditions Kan!(m, k) for any m ≥n + 1, 0 ≤k ≤m. These
conditions are
• Kan(m, k): The horn projection pm
k : hom(∆m, X ) →hom(Λm
k , X ) is surjective.
• Kan!(m, k): The horn projection pm
k : hom(∆m, X ) →hom(Λm
k , X ) is bijective.
An ∞-groupoid is also known as a Kan complex. An n-group is an n-groupoid
X with X0 = pt. We say an ∞-groupoid has order n if it is an n-groupoid but
not an (n −1)-groupoid.
Intuitively, the Kan condition Kan(m, k) states the following: For any configu-
ration of (m −1)-simplices in X forming an (m, k)-horn, that is, an m-tuple as in
(1.21), there exists a horn filler, which is an m-simplex x ∈Xm such that dix = xi
for any i ̸= k. For Kan!(m, k), this horn filler is unique.
Example 1.2.5. Every set X has an associated identity groupoid X with X at each
level and all simplicial maps equal to the identity of X. This is a 0-groupoid. This
defines an embedding Set ,→SSet which consists in associating each set to the
constant functor at that set.
Moreover, any 0-groupoid X has all levels isomorphic to X0 and all its sim-
plicial maps are isomorphisms. In fact, by using the simplicial identities, it can
be shown inductively that, at every level m, dm
i
= ϕm for any 0 ≤i ≤m and
sm
i
= (ϕm)−1 for any 0 ≤i ≤m −1 for some isomorphism ϕm : Xm →Xm−1.
Hence 0-groupoids are identified with sets.
Intuitively, this corresponds to the fact that if each 1-horn (i.e. a point) identi-
fies a unique arrow ending in it and a unique arrow starting at it, these two must
be the same, and this construction is the same as that of the nerve of the discrete
category of a set.

Example 1.2.6. A groupoid X1 ⇒X0 in the usual sense of small category where
all arrows are invertible, is precisely a 1-groupoid in the sense of definition 1.2.4,
when identified with its nerve as defined in Example 1.1.9. Even more, a category
is a groupoid if and only if its nerve is a Kan complex, as shown for example in
[Lur24, Tag 0037].
In our convention, the source and target maps are s = d1

X (2) →X1, where we write (g, h) ∈X (2) if t(g) = s(h) and m(g, h) = h · g.
In this example, because of the strict Kan conditions starting at level 2, every 2-
horn can be uniquely filled. This means that for any two arrows with a common
endpoint, there exist a third one, which is identified with the third face of the
unique filler. Thus, the (2, 1)-horn fillers are defining the multiplication and the
(2, 0)- or (2, 2)-horn fillers are defining the inversion maps, and viceversa. Going
higher, the 3-horn fillers correspond to associativity. This finiteness of the data
needed to describe a 1-groupoid is a general feature of all n-groupoids that we
discuss in Section 1.2.5.
Kan complexes have two important properties:
• Homotopy of simplices is an equivalence relation. See e.g. [May67, Prop.
3.2]. This allows to define homotopy groups for a Kan complex X , relative
to a vertex p ∈X0 by setting
πn(X , p) = {X ∈Xn | di(X) = 1p,
∀0 ≤i ≤n}/ ∼,
for any n ≥1, where X ∼Y if there exists an (n + 1)-simplex W such that
d0W = X, d1W = Y, and diW = 1p for all 2 ≤i ≤n + 1. Similarly, the set
of connected components of X is
π0(X ) = {p ∈X0}/ ∼,

with the same equivalence relation above. These πn(X ) for n ≥1 have
group structures as defined for example in [May67, §I.4].
• Any mapping space Maps(X , Y) where Y is a Kan complex is a Kan com-
plex. See e.g. [May67, Thm. 6.9], [GJ09, Cor. I.5.3], [Lur24, Tag 00TJ].
Specializing the first property to n-groupoids, we have that if X is an n-group-
oid, then its homotopy groups for m > n are trivial: πm(X , p) = {1p} for all
m > n. This follows immediately by the strict Kan conditions at level m > n, as
because of them, the only m-simplex in Xm with all faces equal to 1p ∈Xm−1 is
exactly 1p ∈Xm. We make the following definitions.
Definition 1.2.7. A map f : X →Y between two simplicial sets is called a ho-
motopy equivalence if there exist a map g : Y →X such that g ◦f ≃idX and
f ◦g ≃idY. That is, there exist simplicial homotopies as defined in Definition
1.1.23 between each composition and the respective identity.
A map f : X →Y between two Kan complexes X and Y is called a weak
equivalence if it induces isomorphisms on all homotopy groups, that is

We say a Kan complex X is an n-type if the homotopy groups πm(X , p) are
trivial for any m > n and any p ∈X0, and non-trivial for m = n. If X is a 0-type
and π0(X ) = pt, we say X is contractible.
Example 1.2.8. Any n-groupoid is at most an n-type. In particular, the identity
groupoid of any set is a 0-type. The pair groupoid of a set is contractible.
Remark 1.2.9. Homotopy equivalences of Kan complexes are the same as weak
equivalences. This is a simplicial version of the Whitehead theorem that can be
found for example in [Lur24, Tag 00V2]. With this, n-types should be thought of
as “n-groupoids up to homotopy”.
As for the second property, we could not find an improved version of this re-
sult for n-groupoids in the literature, so we write one here, by assembling various
other results. This result previously appeared in [RZ24].
Theorem 1.2.10. Let X be a simplicial set, and Y be an n-groupoid. Then Maps(X , Y)
is an n-groupoid.
Proof. First of all, by the aforementioned [May67, Thm. 6.9], Maps(X , Y) is an
∞-groupoid. Thus we only need to prove the strict Kan condition Kan(m, k)!
for m ≥n + 1. To prove this, we use the theory of anodyne extensions and the
standard simplicial model structure on the category of simplicial sets, reviewed
in e.g. [GJ09, Ch. I]. A fibration (or Kan fibration) of simplicial sets is a map
with the right lifting property with respect to trivial cofibrations. That is, in the
lifting problem (1.22), fibrations are the maps f : X →Y such that if i is a trivial
cofibration, a lift always exists. In simplicial sets, trivial cofibrations are precisely

1.2.2
Higher Lie groupoids
Let Mfd be the category of (finite-dimensional) smooth manifolds.6 To define
Lie n-groupoids, we first need to discuss spaces of horn-shaped simplicial dia-
grams inside simplicial manifolds. A problem arises here because the sets (1.21)
need not have a canonical smooth manifold structure, as they are constructed
through fiber products (i.e. pullbacks), which are particular limits. Not all fiber
products exist in the category of smooth manifolds Mfd, because of transversality
issues. However, fiber products (and more general limits) always exist in the cat-
egory of sheaves over manifolds with respect to the Grothendieck pretopology
of surjective submersions. In this section we describe how to leverage this to de-
fine certain diagram spaces before knowing whether they exist as manifolds. We
follow [Hen08, §2],[Zhu09, §1, 2],[Li14, §3.2], [Wol16, §2], [RZ20, §3.4, §4.2] as
references for this discussion. A similar approach was also used in [BG17] to dis-
cuss n-groupoids in descent categories, which unlike Mfd, are required to have
all finite limits. The discussion that follows is phrased in terms of the category of
smooth manifolds for the sake of concreteness, but everything works for any cat-
egory with a Grothendieck pretopology satisfying the assumptions in the above
references.
Definition 1.2.11. Consider the Yoneda embedding
y : Mfd ,→PSh(Mfd)
M 7→yM := Mfd(_, M),
of the category of manifolds into the category of presheaves over manifolds (i.e.
functors Mfdop →Set). We say a presheaf F is representable if F = yM for
some M.
5A particular kind of anodyne extensions is given by the collapsible extensions we consider in
Section 1.2.3. We also give a definition of Kan fibrations of simplicial manifolds in 1.2.38.
6This discussion can be adapted to simplicial Banach manifolds as in the work of [Hen08;
Zhu09; RZ20], but since we will be discussing finite-dimensional vector bundles and their duals,
we have no need for this higher generality.


## Page 38


20
Background Notions
The category of smooth manifolds has a specific structure that makes it so
that every representable presheaf on Mfd is actually a sheaf: the subcanonical
Grothendieck pretopology of surjective submersions. Recall that to talk about
sheaves on a category C one needs to introduce the notion of a Grothendieck
pretopology. This is a collection T of morphisms in C such that it contains iso-
morphisms and it is closed under compositions and pullbacks.7 Additionally we
assume that it contains the canonical maps from any object to the terminal object
of the category ([Hen08, Def. 2.1], [RZ20, Def. 3.1]). Morphisms in the class T
are called covers.
The class of surjective submersions in Mfd is a Grothendieck pretopology. In
fact, pullbacks where one of the maps in the starting diagram is a surjective sub-
mersion exist, and the pullback of a surjective submersion is a surjective submer-
sion, as pictured in the diagram
X
Y
∃X ×Y Z ∈Mfd

⌟
where we denote surjective submersions by ↠. The other properties can be triv-
ially checked.
Additionally, surjective submersions form a subcanonical pretopology, which
means that all representable presheaves over Mfd are actually sheaves. That is,
every presheaf F ∈PSh(Mfd) such that F = yM for some M ∈Mfd, satis-
fies the descent condition: for every surjective submersion U ↠X, F(X) is the
equalizer of the diagram
F(U)
F(U ×X U).
We will henceforth consider the Yoneda embedding as a map y : Mfd ,→
Sh(Mfd), and consider limits in Mfd a priori as sheaves. As anticipated, this works
firstly because the category of sheaves is complete. So even if the limit of a di-
agram does not exist in Mfd, the limit of the corresponding embedded diagram
still exists in Sh(Mfd). Secondly, the Yoneda embedding preserves and reflects
limits ([Rie16, Thm. 3.4.6]). Hence, a limit of a diagram of representable sheaves
in Sh(Mfd) is representable if and only if the original diagram had a limit in Mfd,
which is the representing object. For more on pretopologies and other related
technicalities we refer to [RZ20, §3.2].
With this we can define objects of simplicial diagrams for simplicial mani-
folds.

manifold. The object of S-shaped simplicial diagrams in X is the sheaf
hom(S, X )(U) := SMfd(U ⊗S, X ) ∈Sh(Mfd),

where U ⊗S is the copowering (Definition 1.1.20) of the identity groupoid of U
with S. When this sheaf is representable we denote the representing object by

The discrete simplicial manifold S can more formally be written as pt ⊗S,

We can now describe horn filling problems for simplicial manifolds, leading
to the definition of Lie n-groupoids.
Definition 1.2.16. Let n be a non-negative integer or ∞. A Lie n-groupoid X
is a simplicial manifold satisfying the Kan conditions Kan(m, k) for any m ≥1,
0 ≤k ≤m and the strict Kan conditions Kan!(m, k) for any m ≥n + 1, 0 ≤k ≤m.
These conditions are
• Kan(m, k): The horn space hom(Λm

• Kan!(m, k): The horn space hom(Λm
k , X ) is representable and the projection
pm
k : hom(∆m, X ) →hom(Λm
k , X ) is a diffeomorphism.
A Lie n-group is a Lie n-groupoid X with X0 = pt.
We say that a Lie ∞-groupoid has order n if it is a Lie n-groupoid but not an
(n −1)-groupoid.
Contrary to the case of the standard simplex, the fact that hom(Λk

manifold is not trivial. As such it is required to hold in the definition of Kan
conditions. A posteriori, each of these sheaves for a fixed level k is representable
if the Kan conditions hold at lower levels, by an inductive argument. We discuss
this in the next section together with a formula to compute the dimension of the
horn spaces.

1.2.3
Representability and dimension of the horn spaces
In this section we compute the dimension of the horn spaces of a Lie ∞-groupoid.
More generally, we compute the dimension of any object hom(S, X ) where S is
a generalized horn or union of faces, as we now define. The definition of gener-
alized horns is taken from [Joy08, §2.2.1], while that of union of faces is inspired
by the approach of [BG17]. The difference is only notational, as they both de-
note the same object. Nevertheless, we find it helpful to keep both perspectives
in mind.
Recall that, by (1.12), the j-th k-face of the (k + 1) simplex can be recovered
as either the image of the face inclusion δj : ∆k →∆k+1, denoted by δj∆k, or
the simplicial subset of ∆k+1 generated by djEk+1, which we denote by dj∆k+1 ⊆
∆k+1. The latter can also be defined as the simplicial subset of all simplices not
containing the vertex j, that is
(dj∆k+1)m := { f : [m] →[k + 1] | Im f ⊉{j}}.
In geometric terms, this is the k-dimensional face opposite to the vertex j. With
this notation, ∆k ∼= dj∆k+1 ∼= δj∆k for every j ∈{0, . . . , k}.

Collapsible extensions
In this section, we introduce collapsible extensions as defined in [Li14, §2.6],
[RZ20, Definition 3.5].9 For a general category with a Grothendieck pretopol-
ogy (C, T), this is a class of maps between simplicial sets that correspond to cov-
ers under the functor hom(_, X ) when X is an ∞-groupoid object. We recall
9Collapsible extensions are also called expansions in [Wol16] and [BG17].


## Page 42


24
Background Notions
this in Lemma 1.2.28, which appears in [Li14, Lemma 3.20], [RZ20, Lemma 3.7],
and which is the fundamental result needed to prove that horn spaces are repre-
sentable. In the context of groupoids in descent categories Lemma 1.2.28 appears
as Lemma 3.9 in [BG17]. Collapsible extensions are a subclass of the class of an-
odyne extensions, from [GZ67, §IV.2], [GJ09, §I.4], [Joy08, §2.2]. For example, the
latter class additionally contains retracts of collapsible extensions. Anodyne ex-
tensions are trivial cofibrations in the standard model category of simplicial sets
(see [GJ09]), so [Hen08, Corollary 2.11] is yet another (more general) version of
1.2.28.
There are two reasons why we need collapsible extensions. Together with the
notion of collapsible simplicial sets, which they extend, they are helpful to solve
the issue of representability of horn spaces, as we report in Lemma 1.2.30. More
importantly for computing dimensions, as we recall in Lemma 1.2.32, there is
a formula computing the dimension of fiber products of manifolds where both
maps in the starting diagram are surjective submersions. Collapsible extensions
describe exactly the dual situation of this, as a sequence of pushouts over col-
lapsible extensions corresponds to a sequence of fiber products over surjective
submersions through the functor hom(_, X ).
Definition 1.2.20. Let S ,→T be an inclusion of (levelwise finite) simplicial sets.
We call S ,→T a collapsible extension if there is a finite filtration of simplicial
sets

We now report the main lemma relating collapsible extensions and covers
from [Li14, Lemma 3.20] and [RZ20, Lemma 3.7].
Lemma 1.2.28. Let S →T be a collapsible extension of simplicial sets and let X be an
∞-groupoid in (C, T) with the usual assumptions. Assume that hom(S, X ) is repre-
sentable. Then hom(T , X ) is representable, and the map hom(T , X ) →hom(S, X )
is a cover.

As a consequence of this lemma, horn spaces of increasingly higher dimen-
sion are inductively representable. For higher Lie groups this is [Hen08, Lemma
2.4, Corollary 2.5]. The result for general higher groupoids in (C, T) is contained
in [RZ20, Lemma 3.9, Remark 3.10]. We report a specific version of this result
here and refer to these references for a more general result and a complete proof.
Lemma 1.2.30. Let X be a simplicial object in (C, T). If X satisfies Kan(l, j) for all
1 ≤l < m and 0 ≤j ≤l, then hom(Λm
k , X ) is representable for all 0 ≤k ≤m and
we denote it by Λm
k (X ).
In particular all the horn spaces of a Lie n-groupoid for any n are repre-
sentable.
Remark 1.2.31. Lemma 1.2.30 uses the fact that the horn is a collapsible subset of
the standard simplex, as in Definition 1.2.20. On the contrary, the boundary ∂∆n
is not collapsible, so the same results cannot be applied to show representability
of boundary spaces. In fact, the sheaf hom(∂∆n, X ) is a priori not representable.
For example consider hom(∂∆2, X ). This can be written as the fiber product
Λ2
2(X ) ×(d1d0,d1d1),X0×X0,(d0,d1) X1.
Even if X is a Lie groupoid, the fact that either of the maps in the fiber product is
a surjective submersion is not guaranteed. For example, if (d0, d1) is a surjective
submersion, the Lie groupoid is said to be locally trivial [Mac05, Def. 1.3.2] and
it is transitive, but not all Lie groupoids are transitive. In a similar way, we cannot
conclude that (d1d0, d1d1) is a surjective submersion from the fact that the face
maps are surjective submersions.

on the following lemma about the dimension of fiber products of manifolds over
two surjective submersions.

1.2.4
Weak equivalences and fibrations
To define weak equivalences and fibrations for groupoids we need to use full-
generality lifting problems of the form (1.22). In the proof of Theorem 1.2.10,
we recalled a definition of fibrations in terms of a right lifting property. We now
give a more explicit definition for a general category with a pretopology (C, T),
which is nevertheless equivalent to the previous one for SSet.
General lifting problems define spaces of simplicial diagrams along a map
f : X →Y, which are also known as objects of commutative squares in [Hen08],
[Zhu09]. These can also be seen as objects of simplicial diagrams in the arrow
category [Li14, §2.3.1].
Definition 1.2.36. Let u : S →T be a simplicial map between simplicial sets
S, T and f : X →Y be a simplicial map between simplicial manifolds X , Y.

Remark 1.2.37. Objects of commutative squares are limits: they can be written
as fiber products

f→Y) might be representable

of just writing it as a fiber product. The fiber product description is nevertheless

of the underlying sets of its factors, as in Remark 1.2.14.
The lifting problem (1.22) with S →T the horn inclusion Λm
j ,→∆m is the
problem of existence of relative horn fillers — that is, horn fillers in X with a pre-
scribed image through f in Y — and it leads to the notion of Kan fibrations. These
are useful to characterize simplicial vector bundles, as we discuss in Proposition
1.4.4.
Definition 1.2.38. Let f : X →Y be a simplicial map between simplicial objects
in (C, T). We say f satisfies the Kan condition Kan(m, j) if the object of commu-
tative squares hom(Λm
j →∆m, X
f→Y) is representable and the canonical map
hom(∆m, X ) = Xm
(pm
j ,f )

j →∆m, X
f→Y)
is a cover. If this map is additionally an isomorphism, we say that f satisfies the
strict Kan condition Kan!(m, j).
We say f is a Kan fibration if it satisfies Kan(m, j) for all m ≥1, 0 ≤j ≤m.
Additionally we say it is n-strict if it satisfies Kan!(m, j) for all m ≥n + 1. We say
a Kan fibration has order n if it is n-strict but not (n −1)-strict.

Remark 1.2.40. Similarly to the case of the horn space of an ∞-groupoid object
in (C, T), which is inductively representable, a general version of Lemma 1.2.30
shows that if f satisfies Kan(m, j) for all m < l, then hom(Λl

representable. We refer to [Hen08, Lemma 2.4], [Li14, Lemma 3.29] or [RZ20,
Lemma 3.9] for a proof. For more results on Kan fibrations of simplicial objects
and n-groupoid objects see for example [Li14, §3.4].
Remark 1.2.41. As remarked in [HT24, Def. 2.4], the order of a composition of
Kan fibrations is the maximum between the orders of the two fibrations. There-
fore, if Y is an n-groupoid object of order n and f : X →Y is a Kan fibration of
order m, then X is a k-groupoid of order k = max{n, m}.
For a map to be a weak equivalence of simplicial objects in a concrete cate-
gory (C, T) it is not enough to require that the underlying map of simplicial sets
is a weak equivalence. This has to do with the fact that the forgetful functor is
generally not conservative: the fact that the underlying map of f ∈Mor(C) is a
bijection does not always imply that f is an isomorphism in C. This is the case
in Mfd. On the other hand, because the forgetful functor of Vec is monadic, it is
also conservative (see e.g. [Rie16, Ch. 5]). In fact, as we see in Section 1.3, weak
equivalences of SVec are indeed defined as weak equivalences of the underlying
simplicial sets. The way to resolve this problem is to choose a collection of point
functors that is large enough to detect isomorphims. We briefly recall the exposi-
tion in [RZ20, §4] and subsequent sections. As the authors point out, this notion
originated in topos theory (see e.g. [Joh02, p. C.2.2], [MM94, §VII.5]), and was
particularly used for homotopy theory of simplicial sheaves over smooth mani-
folds in e.g. [Dug98] and [NSS15].
Definition 1.2.42 ([RZ20, Def. 4.1]). Let (C, T) be a category equipped with a
pretopology. A point of (C, T) is a functor
p : Sh(C) →Set
which preserves finite limits and small colimits.
A collection of points P of (C, T) is jointly conservative if a morphsims of
sheaves f : F →G in Sh(C) is an isomorphism if and only if for all p ∈P,
pf : pF →pG


## Page 54


36
Background Notions
is an isomorphism of Set. The set pF is also called the p-stalk of the sheaf F.
If the category C is concrete with respect to U : C →Set, we say that P con-
tains the forgetful functor if there exists a p0 ∈P such that p0y = U, with y the
Yoneda embedding.
For any simplicial object X in (C, T), we denote by pX the simplicial object
pyX , where here y denotes the Yoneda embedding to simplicial sheaves over C
defined as for manifolds in (1.24).
Example 1.2.43. A jointly conservative collection of points for Set or for Vec is
simply given by evaluation on the point pt or R, respectively. Hence, by Remark
1.1.3, such a collection contains the forgetful functor. A jointly conservative col-
lection of points for smooth manifolds was described in [Dug98, Def. 3.4.6]:
Denote by Bn
k the n-ball of radius 1
k centered at the origin in Rn. Then we define
the point pn for any n ≥0 as
pn(F) = colimk→∞F(Bn
k ),
for any F ∈SSh(Mfd). The collection P = {pn}n≥0 is a collection of points, where
p0y is the forgetful functor. An extension of this collection for the category of
Banach manifolds was defined in [RZ20, §4.1], where it was shown to be jointly
conservative. Since this restricts to P over Mfd, this is also jointly conservative.
We summarize some results from [RZ20, §4.2] that we find helpful to bridge
the gap between the theory of groupoids in Set and in (C, T), by understand-
ing how the latter is generalizing the first one stalkwise. The definition of weak
equivalences of simplicial objects in (C, T) is also a step in this direction.
Remark 1.2.44. Let (C, T, P) be a category with a pretopology and a jointly con-
servative collection of points. By applying py to a cover f ∈T, the obtained map
pf∗is a surjection for any p ∈P. In other words, any cover in T is a stalkwise
surjection for any collection of points P. As pointed out in [RZ20, Rem. 6.10] for
the case of surjective submersions in Mfd, stalkwise surjections are still a weaker
notion than covers in general.
Remark 1.2.45. Let (C, T, P) be a category with a pretopology and a jointly con-
servative collection of points. In a similar way as for covers, if f : X →Y is a
Kan fibration of simplicial objects in (C, T) then for any point functor p ∈P, pf is
a Kan fibration of simplicial sets. Therefore, a Kan fibration in (C, T) is in a sense
a stalkwise Kan fibration for any collection of points P on (C, T). In particular, if X
is an n-groupoid object in (C, T), pX is an n-groupoid in Set for any point functor
p. It follows from the same observation as in the previous remark that generally
the notion of Kan fibration in (C, T) is stronger than the notion of stalkwise Kan
fibration.
Definition 1.2.46 ([RZ20, p. 5.1]). Let (C, T, P) be a category with a pretopology
and a jointly conservative collection of points. We say a morphism f : X →Y of
simplicial objects in C is a stalkwise weak equivalence if, for any p ∈P,
pf : pX →pY,
is a weak equivalence of simplicial sets.


## Page 55


1.2 Higher Groupoids
37
Remark 1.2.47. In [RZ20, §5.1], the authors prove that this definition is com-
patible with the construction of simplicial homotopy group sheaves of n-groups
appearing in [Hen08], in the sense that a morphism of n-groups that induces
an isomorphism between the simplicial homotopy groups is a stalkwise weak
equivalence, just as for SSet.
In [RZ20], the reason for assigning the stalkwise weak equivalences to be the
weak equivalences is that of showing that with this assignment, the category of
∞-groupoids in (C, T) is an incomplete category of fibrant objects (iCFO) if T is
locally stalkwise ([RZ20, Def. 6.5]) with respect to the collection of points P. The
details of this fact are outside the scope of this thesis, so we refer to their paper
for more information. The main points we are interested in are that: First of all,
this is the case for Mfd with surjective submersions and the collection of points
defined above. Secondly, we need the following characterization of stalkwise
weak equivalences in (Mfd, Ts.sub., P). A version of this result for CFOs of higher
geometric groupoids in a descent category appeared previously in [BG17, Thm.
5.1].

Remark 1.2.52. As expected, the class of weak equivalences generates an equiva-

if they are connected by a span of Morita equivalences X

equivalence as a span of hypercovers. In practice, all Morita equivalences ap-
pearing in this thesis are given by a single weak equivalence instead of a more
general span.
1.2.5
Coskeleta and Finite Data
Because of the Kan conditions, n-groupoid objects in any category with a pre-
topology (C, T) can be reduced from an infinite simplicial object to a finite set of
data. For a Lie 1-groupoid as in Example 1.2.6, this can be thought of as an in-
verse of the nerve construction. In this section we review the coskeleton functor
and how to use its properties to obtain the finite data of an n-groupoid object.
We focus particularly on 2-groupoid objects and introduce a helpful notation for
their higher multiplications. Our main references for this section are [Dus01],
[Zhu09]. See also [Stacks, Tag 0AMA] and [Lur24, Tag 0513]. Throughout this
section we consider simplicial objects in a concrete category (C, T) with a locally
stalkwise pretopology with respect to a collection of points P which contains the
forgetful functor (see Definition 1.2.42). The main example is the category of
smooth manifolds with surjective submersions as covers and the collection of
points described in Example 1.2.43.
Consider the m-truncation of a simplicial object X , which is obtained by “for-
getting” all simplicial levels higher than Xm. This consists of X0, . . . , Xm and face
and degeneracy maps between them. We denote it by trm X . In other words a
truncated simplicial object is a functor ∆op
≤m →C, where ∆≤m is the full subcat-
egory of ∆generated by the elements [0], . . . , [m]. This defines a functor trm from
the category SC of simplicial objects in C to the category SC≤m of m-truncated
simplicial objects in C.
The truncation trm admits a left adjoint skm called the skeleton functor:
SC(skm s
X , Y) ∼= SC≤m( s
X , trmY),
∀s
X ∈SC≤m, ∀Y ∈SC.
The skeleton skm s
X is then easily described by being the simplicial object con-
sisting only of higher degeneracies of simplices in s
X in all levels higher than
m. When composed with the truncation functor, it gives an endofunctor Skm :=
skm trm : SC →SC. In other words, the skeleton of a simplicial object X is its sim-
plicial subobject generated by all simplices of dimension lower than m and their
higher degeneracies. It is common practice to identify each m-truncated simpli-
cial object with its m-skeleton so that one can get away with considering only
simplicial objects instead of m-truncated ones. In this picture, any m-truncated
simplicial object corresponds to an m-skeletal simplicial object, i.e. one that is
isomorphic to its m-skeleton.
Definition 1.2.53. If it exists, the m-coskeleton Coskm X of a simplicial object
X ∈SC is the representing object of the presheaf
U 7→SC(Skm U, X ),
∀U ∈SC.

Clearly, by this definition, if the m-coskeleton is defined for all simplicial ob-
jects in C, then it is a functor (called coskeleton functor), which is the right ad-
joint of Skm.
Remark 1.2.54. A coskeleton functor can also be defined as coskm : SC≤m →SC.
This is the right adjoint of trm as follows. By the identification of m-truncated
and m-skeletal simplicial objects, if the coskeleton of X = Skm X exists, then the
representing object of the presheaf
U 7→SC≤m(trm U, trm X )
is coskm(trm(X )) = Coskm(Skm(X )). Now if all coskeleta exist, there is an ad-
junction skm ⊣trm ⊣coskm, between SC and SC≤m.
Lemma 1.2.55 ([Stacks, Tag 0183]). If the category C has finite limits, then coskm
and Coskm functors exist for all m. Moreover, each level of the coskeleton of any (m-
truncated) simplicial object can be written as a limit in C. This can be written recursively

all coskeleta of simplicial sets exist and Coskm defines a functor. This is also true
for Vec.
In the category of simplicial manifolds, not all coskeleta exist a priori because
the iterated boundary spaces in (1.38) are not always representable in Mfd, as
we observed in Remark 1.2.31. In this case, and in any other non-complete cat-
egories, we can consider Coskm(X ) to be a priori defined as a simplicial sheaf.
That is, we see it as an object in SSh(C). If this sheaf is representable, we identify
it with its representing object and denote both by Coskm(X ).

Proof. By definition of coskeleton we have that for any l,

If l ≤m, then Skm ∆l = ∆l, so this is just Xl. If l ≥m + 1, then Skm ∆l = Skm ∂∆l,
so that

If l = m + 1 we have the special case where the latter is SSet(∂∆m+1, X ).
The simplicial maps defined in the statement are then the obvious canonical
choices that satisfy the simplicial identities.
We now recall that all coskeleta of simplicial objects satisfy certain strict Kan
conditions and that conversely, simplicial sets which satisfy certain strict Kan
conditions are isomorphic to their m-coskeleton for some m. Such simplicial sets
are called m-coskeletal. A proof of this fact can be found for example in [Li14,
Prop. 2.37].
Lemma 1.2.57 ([Li14, Prop. 2.37]). Let X be a simplicial set. Then
(i) If X is m-coskeletal, then X satisfies Kan!(l, j) for all l ≥m + 2 and 0 ≤j ≤m.
(ii) If X satisfies Kan!(l, j) for all l ≥m, then X is m-coskeletal. In particular, if X
is an n-groupoid, then it is n + 1-coskeletal.
We now extend this property to general simplicial objects in (C, T, P), where
the m-coskeleton might not be representable a priori.
Definition 1.2.58. Let (C, T, P) be a category with a pretopology and a jointly
conservative collection of points. A simplicial object X in C is stalkwise m-
coskeletal if pX ∼= Coskm(pX ) for any p ∈P. A simplicial object X in C is
weakly stalkwise m-coskeletal if pX ⊆Coskm(pX ) for any p ∈P.
As an immediate consequence of the collection of points P being jointly con-
servative, we have the following lemma.
Lemma 1.2.59. A simplicial object X is stalkwise m-coskeletal if and only if Coskm(X )
exists (i.e. it is representable as a simplicial sheaf) and it is isomorphic to X . In this case
we say that X is m-coskeletal.


## Page 60


42
Background Notions
We also note that if X is weakly stalkwise m-coskeletal in a concrete category
whose collection of points contains the forgetful functor, then X can be seen as
a subset of its m-coskeleton with the structure of an object in C. For example a
weakly m-coskeletal simplicial manifold is a simplicial subset of its m-coskeleton
that is a smooth simplicial manifold.
Lemma 1.2.60. All n-groupoid objects in (C, T, P) are (n + 1)-coskeletal and weakly
stalkwise n-coskeletal. That is, if X is an n-groupoid object in (C, T, P), then
X ∼= Coskn+1(X ), and pX ⊆Coskn(pX ), ∀p ∈P.
Proof. If X is an n-groupoid in (C, T, P), then pX is an n-groupoid in Set. There-
fore, by 1.2.57, pX is (n + 1)-coskeletal for any p ∈P. In other words, X is
stalkwise n-coskeletal, which implies it is n-coskeletal.
The second part follows from the description of Coskn(pX ) in Lemma 1.2.56,
and the fact that any (n + 1)-simplex in pX ∼= Λn+1
j
(pX ) can be identified with
the boundary of its unique horn filler by Kan!(n + 1, j), which is then an ele-
ment of ∂n+1(X ) = Coskn(X )n+1. The same happens at higher levels, and this
inclusion is clearly compatible with the simplicial maps.
This lemma effectively reduces the data required to describe an n-groupoid
object X to just its (n + 1)-skeleton, (or truncation), since we have
X ∼= Coskn+1(Skn+1(X )).
Additionally, by the strict Kan conditions at level n + 1 and the weak n-coskele-
tality of X , this data can be further reduced by the following construction.
Let X be an n-groupoid object in (C, T, P) with P containing the forgetful
functor. As in the proof of the previous lemma, any (n + 1)-simplex X in X can
be identified with its boundary. Denote the projection (d0, . . . , dn+1) : Xn+1 →

There is one more condition we need, in order to recover the data of a Lie
n-groupoid from its n-truncations and a set of multiplications satisfying the con-
ditions in Remarks 1.2.62 and 1.2.63. This is an associativity condition that comes
from the fact that X satisfies the strict Kan conditions at level n + 2. These
involve (n + 2)-simplices of an n-groupoid, which can be seen as elements of
Coskn+1(X )n+2, and as such can be represented by simplicial matrices. We re-
call this notion following [Gle82; Dus01]. Let X be a simplicial set and m ≥0.
By Lemma 1.2.56, the elements of Coskm Xm+2 are (m + 3) × (m + 2) matrices
(xji) with entries xji ∈Xn such that:
(i) the index 0 ≤j ≤m + 3 denotes the row and 0 ≤i ≤m + 2 the column.
(ii) dkxji = di−1xjk for any 0 ≤j ≤n + 2 and 0 ≤i < k ≤n + 1. That is,
each row is the boundary of an (n + 1)-simplex, an element of ∂m+1(X ) =
Coskm Xm+1.
(iii) xji = xi(j−1), for any 0 ≤i < j ≤n + 2. Denoting the matrix by M this is
the same as the condition that didjM = dj−1diM for any 0 ≤i < j ≤n + 2.

We conclude this section by showing that the finite data construction extends
to simplicial maps with target an n-groupoid object, which are then determined
by their n-truncation, subject to some conditions at level n. This is a fundamental
property that we will use repeatedly in the rest of the thesis.
