1.3
Simplicial vector spaces
In this section we focus on simplicial vector spaces, with a more specific atten-
tion to levelwise finite-dimensional ones over a field of characteristic zero which
is assumed to be R for simplicity. Most of the material we discuss works for sim-
plicial objects in other abelian categories such as abelian groups and modules


## Page 68


50
Background Notions
over a more general ring, with the appropriate considerations. The content of
this section is mostly a rewriting of that contained in the preprint [RZ24].
The definition of space of simplicial diagrams of shape S inside a simplicial
vector space translates directly from that of simplicial sets in Definition 1.2.1 be-
cause all limits exist in Vec and they are even created by the forgetful functor U,
which forms the monadic adjunction R[_] ⊣U with the free vector space functor
R[_].
Definition 1.3.1. Let V be a simplicial vector space. The space of simplicial di-
agrams of shape S in V is
hom(S, V) := SSet(S, U(V)) = SVec(R[S], V),
with the "pointwise" vector space structure inherited from V.
Remark 1.3.2. The functor hom is level 0 of the powering functor hom : SSet ×
SVec →SVec constructed as in Remark 1.2.2. With this, SVec is powered over
SSet.
With this definition we have the vector spaces of m-simplices hom(∆m, V) =
Vm, m-boundaries hom(∂∆m, V) = ∂∆m(V), and (m, k)-horns hom(Λm
k , V) =
Λm
k (V), whose underlying sets are defined as in (1.21). It is also evident that
the Kan conditions in Definition 1.2.4 translate immediately to the category of
simplicial vector spaces, where the horn projections are additionally linear.
Definition 1.3.3. A VS n-groupoid V is a simplicial vector space whose underly-
ing set is an n-groupoid. A VS n-group V is a VS n-groupoid with V0 = 0.
Simplicial vector spaces are in particular simplicial groups, and it is a general
fact that these are always ∞-groupoids, because one can construct arbitrary fillers
in each dimension by using the group operations. This result is due to Moore
[Moo54, Thm. 3 p. 18-04], see also [May67, Thm. 17.1], [Cur71, Lem. 3.1],
[Wei94, Lem. 8.2.8], [GJ09, Lem. I.3.4]. We recall it here and give an explicit
proof in the case of vector spaces, adapted from [nLa24], as it will be useful for
our later discussion. This version is also a particular case of [Wol16, prop. 6.6],
which applies to the more general setting of simplicial Lie groups.
Proposition 1.3.4 (Moore). Simplicial groups are Kan complexes. In particular, sim-
plicial vector spaces are VS ∞-groupoids.
Proof. Let V be a simplicial vector space, and (x0, . . . , pxk, . . . , xn) ∈Λn

induction on 0 ≤i ≤k −1, assume that wi−1 has been defined in a way such that

by a pair of vector spaces with source, target and unit maps between them, as
the multiplication and inversion can be inferred from these. We state a general
version of this fact in Proposition 1.3.23.
1.3.1
Closed monoidal structures and the Dold-Kan correspondence
The Dold-Kan correspondence establishes an equivalence of categories between
the category of simplicial vector spaces SVec and that of non-negative15 chain
complexes Ch≥0(Vec) (see for example [Wei94, §8.4], [May67, §22], [GJ09, §III.2],
[Lur24, Tag 00QQ]). Similarly to SSet, as discussed in Section 1.1.2, Each of these
categories has a closed symmetric monoidal structure. In other words, they are
equipped with a tensor product which admits a natural symmetry isomorphism
and a right adjoint defining internal hom spaces. The tensor-hom adjunction
also holds in both categories in the enriched sense, by replacing the hom functor
with the internal hom as in Proposition 1.1.26. This causes it to descend to an
adjunction in the respective homotopy categories as well.
The simplicial category of simplicial vector spaces and its homotopy category
The monoidal product ⊗in SVec is given levelwise by the tensor product of vec-
tor spaces. For any two simplicial vector spaces V and W, the face and degen-
eracy maps of V ⊗W are dV

symmetric with respect to the symmetry isomorphism v ⊗w 7→w ⊗v. In ad-
dition, there is a way to “tensor” a simplicial vector space and a simplicial set,
known as copowering, which is defined as in Def. 1.1.20: at each level m and for
any V ∈SVec and K ∈SSet, this is
(V ⊗K)m :=

the unique injection of ordinals that skips i, and the codegeneracy map σi is the
postcomposition with the unique surjection of ordinals that repeats i. Because
each level of Hom(V, W) is a vector space and an immediate computation shows
that the face and degeneracy maps defined in (1.18) are linear, Hom is actually a
functor SVec × SVec →SVec, whose level 0 coincides with the hom space SVec.
Its level 1 is the space of simplicial linear homotopies between simplicial linear
maps, and its higher levels encode increasingly higher homotopies. These can be
composed across different mapping spaces according to the composition defined
in (1.19). With this composition and the obvious identity element, SVec is not
just an SSet-category, as previously remarked, but also an SVec-category.
We can now define the homotopy category hSVec as the category with ob-
jects simplicial vector spaces and with morphisms simplicial homotopy classes
of simplicial linear maps. In other words, the hom spaces in hSVec are given by
π0 Hom, in the simplicial sense. For a general construction of this starting from a
simplicial category see for example [Lur24, Tag 00LW]. In this case the construc-
tion is simplified by the fact that all simplicial vector spaces (including mapping
spaces) are Kan, so homotopy of maps is an equivalence relation which we de-
note by ∼. See for example [GJ09, Lemma I.6.1], [Lur24, Tag 00HC] and [Lur24,
Tag 00M0] for more details. By definition, an isomorphism in the homotopy cat-
egory is precisely a homotopy equivalence, that is a pair of maps f : V →W and
g : W →V such that f g ∼idW and g f ∼idV. In this case we write V ≃W. The
tensor product also descends in a straightforward way to the homotopy category.
Going back to the mapping space Hom, this is right adjoint to the tensor prod-
uct, in the enriched sense, as for simplicial sets in 1.1.26. We write the natural
isomorphism explicitly, as we will need it later on.
Proposition 1.3.6 (Enriched tensor-hom adjunction for SVec). For any U, V, W in
SVec, there are natural isomorphisms of simplicial vector spaces
Hom(U ⊗V, W) ∼= Hom(V, Hom(U, W)) ∼= Hom(U, Hom(V, W)).
(1.58)

The DG-category of non-negative chain complexes and its homotopy category
Consider the category of (bounded but not necessarily non-negative) chain com-
plexes Ch(Vec). Its symmetric monoidal structure is given by the tensor product
defined degreewise as
(A ⊗B)m =
M
p+q=m
Ap ⊗Bq,
for any chain complexes (A, ∂), (B, ∂), with differential defined on homogeneous
elements by ∂(a ⊗b) = ∂a ⊗b + (−1)|a|a ⊗∂b, where |a| denotes the degree of
a. It is important to note that with these definitions, the symmetry isomorphism
A ⊗B →B ⊗A has a sign that follows the Koszul sign rule: a ⊗b 7→(−1)|a||b|b ⊗
a.


## Page 73


1.3 Simplicial vector spaces
55
The mapping complex Hom(A, B) is the chain complex with degree m the
space of degree m maps of the underlying graded vector spaces
Hom(A, B)m = { f : A• →B•+m},
and differential ∂f = ∂◦f −(−1)| f | f ◦∂. Clearly this is a chain complex of vector
spaces, as every level is a vector space and the differential is linear. With this, and
the obvious composition rule, Ch(Vec) becomes a category enriched over itself,
also commonly known as a DG-category. See e.g. [Lur24, Tag 00ND].
Note that the mapping complex is generally non-zero in both positive and
negative degrees and that the space of chain maps is given by the space of 0-
cycles:
Ch(Vec)(A, B) = { f : A →B | ∂f = f ∂} = ker ∂0.
Furthermore, two chain maps f, g : A →B are homotopic if and only if they
differ by a 1-boundary h ∈Im(∂1) of the mapping complex, i.e. f −g = ∂h + h∂.
This has the immediate consequence that homotopy of chain maps is an equiv-
alence relation which is compatible with composition. Hence we define the ho-
motopy category of chain complexes (see e.g. [Lur24, Tag 00NM]) as the cate-
gory hCh(Vec) with objects chain complexes of vector spaces and morphisms the
chain maps up to homotopy, that is hCh(Vec)(A, B) = H0(Hom(A, B)), for any
two chain complexes A and B. In particular, the definition of an isomorphism in
the homotopy category as an invertible morphism recovers the notion of a chain
homotopy equivalence, i.e. a pair of maps f : A →B and g : B →A such that
f g ∼idB and g f ∼idA, where ∼denotes the homotopy relation. In this case
we will write A ≃B. The monoidal structure also descends to the homotopy
category, because given f, g : A →B homotopic through h and f ′, g′ : A′ →B′
homotopic through k, there is a homotopy between f ⊗f ′ and g ⊗g′ given by
h ⊗f ′ + g ⊗k.16
Consider now the full subcategory of non-negative chain complexes Ch≥0(Vec).
This is a DG-subcategory, but we can also see it as a category enriched over itself
by truncating the mapping complex at 0 and replacing its 0-chains by its 0-cycles.
That is, we define, for any non-negative A and B,
Hom≥0(A, B)i := tr≥0 Hom(A, B)i =

With this, Ch≥0(Vec) is a category enriched over itself. It also admits a homotopy

defined by
hCh≥0(Vec)(A, B) = H0(Hom≥0(A, B)) = H0(Hom(A, B)) = hCh(Vec)(A, B).

The Dold-Kan correspondence and equivalences
We recall the Dold-Kan correspondence, which holds in general for simplicial
objects in abelian categories. For more details we refer to [Wei94, §8.4], [May67,
§22], [GJ09, §III.2], [Lur24, Tag 00QQ], for example.
Definition 1.3.11. Let V be a simplicial vector space. The Moore complex of V
is the non-negative chain complex C(V) with C(V)n = Vn for any n ≥0 and
differential given at each level n by the boundary map

## Page 76


58
Background Notions
Proposition 1.3.13 (Dold-Kan correspondence). The normalized complex functor
N : SVec →Ch≥0(Vec) admits a right inverse functor DK : Ch≥0(Vec) →SVec, and
the two form an equivalence of categories between the category of simplicial vector spaces
and that of non-negative chain complexes. Under this equivalence, simplicial homotopies
h : V ⊗∆1 →W between two simplicial maps f, g : V →W, correspond to chain
homotopies N(h) : N(V) →N(W)[−1] between N( f ) and N(g).
Since the Dold-Kan correspondence sends homotopic maps in SVec to homo-
topic maps in Ch≥0(Vec), it descends to an equivalence of the homotopy cate-
gories as well.
Corollary 1.3.14. The Dold-Kan correspondence induces an equivalence of categories
between the homotopy categories hSVec and hCh≥0(Vec).
As a first consequence, the normalized complex of a simplicial vector space
contains information about its order as a groupoid.
Definition 1.3.15. Let (E, ∂) be a chain complex. We say (E, ∂) has amplitude
(0, n) if it is concentrated in degrees 0 to n, that is Ei = 0 for i < 0 or i > n.
Proposition 1.3.16. A simplicial vector space V is a VS n-groupoid if and only if N(V)
has amplitude (0, n). In particular, the order of a simplicial vector space coincides with
the maximal degree for which its normalized chain complex is non-zero.
Proof. By definition, all normalized complexes are already non-negative. If V is
a VS n-groupoid, then clearly N(V)i = ker pi
i = 0 for any i > n. Hence N(V) has
amplitude (0, n).
For the converse, the theorem of Moore (Theorem 1.3.4) implies that all the
pm

then pm

with inverse the Moore filler µm

all the horns have the same dimension, so the maps pm

are isomorphisms for all m ≥n + 1 and 0 ≤j ≤m. Therefore pm

Definition 1.3.17. We define the homotopy type of a simplicial vector space as
the maximal degree for which the homology of the normalized complex is non-
zero. That is, we say V is of type n — or an n-type — if Hm(N(V)) = 0 for all
m > n and Hn(N(V)) ̸= 0. If Hi(N(V)) = 0 for all i, then we say V is acyclic.
Remark 1.3.18. It follows from the definition and Proposition 1.3.16 that a VS
n-groupoid can only have homotopy type lower or equal to n. Recall (e.g. from
[May67, Thm. 22.1], [GJ09, p. III.2.7]) that for a VS n-groupoid Hi(N(V)) ∼=
πi(V, 0), which can then be shown to be isomorphic to the corresponding homo-
topy group at any other point. Hence the definition of a VS n-type coincides with
that of a set-theoretical n-type in Definition 1.2.7.


## Page 77


1.3 Simplicial vector spaces
59
Example 1.3.19. Any VS 1-groupoid of order 1 can be seen as a VS n-groupoid for
any n ≥1, but it is not an n-groupoid of order n for any n > 1. VS 1-groupoids
can be of homotopy type 1, 0 or be acyclic. VS 2-groupoids can in addition be of
type 2.
Homotopy equivalences and weak equivalences for simplicial vector spaces
can be defined as in Definition 1.2.7, but they have a much simpler characteriza-
tion, due to the Dold-Kan correspondence.
Proposition 1.3.20. In SVec, the category of simplicial vector spaces, the following are
equivalent:
1. f : V →W is a weak equivalence in the sense that it induces isomorphisms
between the homotopy groups at each point and between the sets of connected com-
ponents.
2. f : V →W is a homotopy equivalence in the sense that it admits a simplicial
homotopy inverse.
3. N( f ) : N(V) →N(W) is a quasi-isomorphism of chain complexes, in the
sense that it induces an isomorphism between the respective homologies.
4. N( f ) : N(V) →N(W) is a chain homotopy equivalence, in the sense that it
admits a chain homotopy inverse.
Proof. By the Dold-Kan correspondence being an equivalence between the ho-
motopy categories (Corollary 1.3.14), (2) and (4) are equivalent.
By the discussion in [Wei94, §1.4], (3) and (4) are equivalent: In one direc-
tion it is clear that chain homotopy equivalences are quasi-isomorphisms. In
the other, every chain complex (A, ∂) of vector spaces is split in the sense that
An ∼= Hn(A) ⊕Im ∂n+1 ⊕Im ∂n, which gives a chain homotopy equivalence be-
tween A and its homology H(A) that can then be composed on both sides of any
quasi-isomorphism A →B to upgrade it to a chain homotopy equivalence.
Finally (1) and (3) are equivalent because by the Dold-Kan correspondence,
for any simplicial vector space V, πi(V, 0) ∼= Hi(N(V)) for any i ≥0, as men-
tioned in Remark 1.3.18. In addition, change of basepoint can be accounted for
by using the isomorphism πi(V, 0) →πi(V, p) induced by multiplication by the
unit 1p of each basepoint p ∈V0.
Remark 1.3.21. It is important to note that all of the above equivalences preserve
homotopy type but not order. For example, any n-type V ∈SVec is homotopy
equivalent to DK(H(N(V))), which is always a VS n-groupoid of order n re-
gardless of whether V is an n-groupoid. This is one of the reasons why we do
not restrict our discussion to VS n-groupoids of a certain fixed order, as observed
and expanded upon in Remark 1.3.26.

Proposition 1.3.23 (Finite data for VS n-groupoids). The data of a VS n-groupoid

1.4
Higher vector bundles
We begin with a concrete picture of simplicial vector bundles.
Definition 1.4.1. A simplicial vector bundle V →G is a simplicial object in the
category VB of vector bundles over Mfd with bundle maps (i.e. smooth fiberwise-
linear maps). That is, the base G is a simplicial manifold and each level qm : Vm →
Gm is a vector bundle over Gm, and all simplicial maps of V are bundle maps over
the respective simplicial maps of G. We can represent a simplicial vector bundle
as a diagram
. . .

in which we denote the face maps by ( edi, di) and the degeneracy maps by (esi, si).
Equivalently the bundle projections of each level assemble into a smooth simpli-
cial map q : V →G.
Definition 1.4.2. Let n be a positive integer or ∞. A VB n-groupoid is a simplicial
vector bundle V over a Lie n-groupoid G that is also a Lie n-groupoid. A VB ∞-
groupoid is also called a higher vector bundle.
This definition is equivalent to that of defining VB n-groupoids as n-groupoid
objects in VB with the Grothendieck pretopology of surjective submersions. Sur-
jective submersions between vector bundles are characterized by the following
lemma.
Lemma 1.4.3. Let E →M, F →N be vector bundles. A bundle map (eϕ, ϕ) : E →F
is a surjective submersion if and only if ϕ is a surjective submersion and eϕ f ib : E →ϕ∗F
is surjective over the identity of M.
Proof. This follows from the fact that (up to a choice of splitting) for any vector
bundle E and any point ep ∈E|p, TepE ∼= Ep ⊕TpM, and the tangent map Tep eϕ :
TepE →Teϕ(ep)F splits as (eϕ f ib|p, Tpϕ) after splitting its domain and codomain, as
eϕ is linear along the fibers.
This elementary lemma characterizes surjective submersions of vector bun-
dles as the fiberwise surjective maps whose base map is a surjective submersion.
Here we say (eϕ, ϕ) : E →F is fiberwise surjective if for any x ∈M, the map
eϕ : E|x →F|ϕ(x) is surjective. This is equivalent to the condition in the lemma
that eϕ f ib : E →ϕ∗F is surjective over the identity of M.


## Page 81


1.4 Higher vector bundles
63
The horn spaces of a higher vector bundle are representable, by the same re-
sult as in the case of Lie ∞-groupoids. Moreover, they are vector bundles over
the corresponding horn spaces of the base. This follows from the more general
result in [HT24, Lemma 5.3].
This gives an additional characterization of higher vector bundles which we
report from [HT24, Prop. 5.4]
Proposition 1.4.4. Let V →G be a simplicial vector bundle over the Lie ∞-groupoid
G. The following are equivalent:
(i) V is a Lie ∞-groupoid (i.e. a higher vector bundle)
(ii) q : V →G is a set-theoretical Kan fibration.
(iii) q : V →G is a smooth Kan fibration.
Note that as a consequence of this, unlike for simplicial vector spaces, there
are simplicial vector bundles which are not VB ∞-groupoids. From now on we
will mostly focus on the latter case, that of higher vector bundles, and denote the
category of higher vector bundles over a fixed Lie n-groupoid G, with morphisms
the simplicial maps covering the identity on the base, as VB∞

order of the base G. This excludes the existence of VB m-groupoids over Lie n-
groupoids of order n with m < n.

Because of this isomorphism, either choice of the left or right core is referred

Example 1.4.10. In the case of the tangent VB groupoid of a Lie groupoid G, the
core is the underlying vector bundle of the Lie algebroid A, while the unit anni-
hilator is the conormal bundle to the units G0. The anchor map A →TG0 is given
by the target Td1 if A is the right core, and Td0 if A is the left core. The bracket
is induced from that of vector fields on G1 by the isomorphism of the sections of
A with vector fields over G1 invariant under right or left translations depending
on the choice of core. For example the right translation TRg : d∗

given in terms of the groupoid multiplication by multiplying on the right by the
zero section:
TRga = a · 0g.
The kind of translation appearing in this example can be defined for any VB
groupoid and it appears in the definition of its dual, which has the dual of the
core as its units. Because of the partially defined groupoid multiplication, only
right core elements can be right translated, while only left core elements can be
left translated. As such, the involution −(_)−1 commonly appears in the de-
scription of the dual VB groupoid in terms of right or left core, as for example in
[Mac05, §11.2].
We write the dual VB groupoid in terms of the unit annihilator as it appears
in [Pra88] for now, as this will be the main framework we use when computing
higher duals. Later, in Section 4.2.1 we will consider the cotangent VB groupoid
in terms of the left core. We generalize this discussion of cores in Section 2.3.2
for VS n-groupoids and in Section 3.2.3 for VB n-groupoids.

1.4.2
Generalizations of the Dold-Kan correspondence
The classical Dold-Kan correspondence holds for simplicial vector bundles over
a manifold, so a natural question to ask is whether this can be extended to a cor-
respondence for all simplicial vector bundles. This is a difficult question, which
was answered for VB 1-groupoids in the work of [GM17], and for general higher
VB n-groupoids in [HT24]. We do not need to recall the entire construction here,
but the upshot is that by adding extra structure (a normal weakly flat cleavage)
to a VB m-groupoid over a Lie n-groupoid G (with m ≥n), this is equivalent to a
representation up to homotopy of G concentrated in degrees (0, m). By restrict-
ing morphisms of VB m-groupoids to ones that are weakly flat (i.e. preserve the
cleavage in a sense), this upgrades to an equivalence of categories.
Representations up to homotopy for Lie groupoids were introduced in [AC13],
but they also make sense for general simplicial sets as studied in [AS12]. A more
specific definition for higher Lie groupoids is given in [HT24]. For our purposes
it is enough to think of a representation up to homotopy of a Lie n-groupoid G
as a complex of vector bundles over G0, with extra data given by a sequence of
maps (Ri)i≥0 specifying the representation data.
Starting from a VB n-groupoid with a choice of cleavage, the complex of vec-
tor bundles associated to its representation up to homotopy can be obtained by
a functor analogous to the normalized functor of the Dold-Kan correspondence,
which actually does not depend on the cleavage at all. In fact, the construction of
this functor only uses the fact that the pullback to the units of a VB n-groupoid is
pointwise a VS n-groupoid (see Remark 1.4.7) to extend the normalized functor
for simplicial vector spaces. On the other hand, the choice of cleavage is essen-
tial to determine the extra data Rm. We limit our present discussion to that of
the normalized complex functor. We will also introduce cleavages in Definition
1.4.19 for other purposes, but for a more complete treatment see [HT24].
Definition 1.4.14. Let V →G be a simplicial vector bundle over the simplicial
manifold G. The Moore complex C(V) of V is the non-negative chain complex
of vector bundles over G0 with C(V)m = 1∗Vm, for any m ≥0, and differential

The normalized complex of the tangent n-groupoid of a Lie n-groupoid G is also
called the tangent complex of G and denoted by T G = N(TG).
Fiberwise, at each fixed point p ∈G0, these complexes follow many properties
of the corresponding ones for vector spaces. In particular the homology of the
normalized complex of a higher vector bundle is well-defined at any point p ∈
G0. We denote it by Hi(V)|p := Hi(N(V)|p). As such, we have a natural notion
of pointwise m-types.
Definition 1.4.15. Let V →G be a higher vector bundle over a Lie n-groupoid
G. We say that V is a pointwise m-type if the homology Hi(V)|p vanishes at all
p ∈G0 for i > m, but not for i = m.
Remark 1.4.16. Note that the homology of a complex of vector bundles is gener-
ally not a complex of vector bundles itself, since the pointwise homology we just
defined does not generally have constant rank over the whole base manifold.
Remark 1.4.17. By Remark 1.4.5, the order of a VB n-groupoid is the maximum
between the highest non-vanishing degree of its normalized complex and the or-
der of the base. Clearly, a VB n-groupoid of order n is at most a pointwise n-type.
Unlike for VS n-groupoids, being a pointwise m-type does not guarantee the ex-
istence of a Morita equivalent VB m-groupoid: consider for example the situation
of V being a pointwise m-type higher vector bundle over a Lie n-groupoid G that
is an n-type with n > m.
Remark 1.4.18. Under the generalization of Dold-Kan in [GM17], with a choice
of Ehresmann connection, 20 the tangent VB groupoid of a Lie groupoid G cor-
responds to the adjoint representation up to homotopy of G relative to the con-
nection. Analogously, the cotangent VB 1-groupoid corresponds to the coadjoint
representation up to homotopy of G relative to the Ehresmann connection.
By using the further generalization in [HT24] and applying it to the tangent
VB n-groupoid of a Lie n-groupoid, it is possible to obtain its adjoint represen-
tation. Analogously, by applying it to the n-cotangent VB n-groupoids we con-
struct in this thesis, one finds the coadjoint representation up to homotopy of a
Lie n-groupoid (relative to a cleavage). We plan to study this in future work.
Note also that the dualization of representations up to homotopy of a Lie n-
groupoid G of order n > 1 is not as straightforward as dualizing its chain com-
plex by a shifted dual construction. In fact, a priori, the representation data will
dualize to a representation up to homotopy of the Lie n-groupoid Gop. It is clear
that this is a Morita equivalent Lie n-groupoid to G, but this Morita equivalence
may not be expressible as a single map by which the representation can be pulled
back to G. It is not clear to us at this point how to translate a representation up
to homotopy of Gop to one of G in absence of the inversion map that exists for
n = 1.
20We recall this definition in Def. 4.2.2. See Remark 1.4.20 for its relation to the notion of cleav-
age.

1.4.3
Weak equivalences of VB 2-groupoids
In this section we extend a particular case of [HO20, Thm. 3.5], which states
that a map of VB 1-groupoids is a Morita map if and only if it is Morita on the
bases and it induces a quasi-isomorphism between the normalized complexes:
we show that a map of VB 2-groupoids over the identity on the base is a weak
equivalence if and only if it induces a quasi-isomorphism between the normal-
ized complexes.
We first recall the notion of a cleavage from [HT21].21 These describe choices
of (n, k)-horn fillers for a simplicial vector bundle. This is mostly for convenience,
as we will need to repeatedly choose fillers in the proof of the theorem and we
can do it “uniformly” by a choice of cleavage.
Definition 1.4.19. Let V →G be a higher vector bundle. For any m ≥1 and
0 ≤k ≤m, an (m, k)-cleavage cm,k is a bundle map
cm,k : (pm
k )∗Λm
k (V) −→Vm
that is a section of the (m, k)-horn projection epm
k over the identity of Gm.
An (m, k) cleavage is said to be normal if it is compatible with the degeneracy
maps in the sense that for any m ∈Vm−1|g, and any 0 ≤i ≤m −1,
cm,k(epm
k esiv, sig) = esiv.
(1.75)
This is equivalent to the sub-bundle of degenerate simplices DmV being included
in the image of cm,k.
Remark 1.4.20. We collect here some properties of cleavages:
• Because of the existence of partition functions, normal cleavages for higher
vector bundles always exist. This was shown in [HT21, Prop. 4.5].
• Cleavages generalize Ehresmann connections on a VB groupoid: a normal
(1, 1)-cleavage is an Ehresmann connection in the sense of Definition 4.2.2.
• Because of linearity of cm,k, any horn with all faces equal to zero is always
filled to the zero section.
• The condition (1.75) for a cleavage to be normal is similar to the compati-
bility condition for higher multiplication maps in Remark 1.2.63 and item
(2b) of Proposition 1.2.67. Indeed, a normal (m, k)-cleavage can be seen as
defining a choice of smooth multiplication map for m-simplices by taking
dkcm,k.
• Clearly, by the Kan conditions, VB n-groupoids have unique (m, k)-cleavages
for all m ≥n + 1 and 0 ≤k ≤m.
• By the Moore Theorem (Prop. 1.3.4) and Lemma 1.3.22, any simplicial vec-
tor space has a set of unique normal cleavages given by the Moore fillers
µn
k. The images of these coincide with the degenerate subspaces DnV. An
alternative proof of this fact is in [HT24, Prop. 3.8].

Theorem 1.4.21. Let V →G and V′ →G be VB 2-groupoids over the same base G.
Let f : V →V′ be a simplicial bundle map over the identity of G. The following are

(iii) At any point p ∈G0, the map N( f )|p : N(V)|p →N(V′)|p is a quasi-isomorphism
of chain complexes.

Remark 1.4.22. An important guiding remark for the proof at each level is that
to translate the boundary in V to a boundary over a unit, we are effectively con-
structing a simplicial diagram resembling a prism (i.e. a homotopy) in V each
time.26 These are however not standard prisms ∆l × ∆1, as their triangulations
are different, so they are not isomorphic to prisms as simplicial sets.
In [Li14, Def. 6.25], the simplicial diagrams appearing in (1.35) for the defi-
nition of a weak equivalence are described in terms of joins of simplicial sets, by
writing r f
l for any simplicial map f : X →Y as
r f
l : hom(∆l →∆l ⋆∆0, X →Y) →hom(∂∆l ⋆∆0, X →Y).
(1.77)
The symbol ⋆denotes the join operation, and we have the isomorphisms
∆l+1 ∼= ∆l ⋆∆0, and Λl+1
l+1 ∼= ∂∆l ⋆∆0.
Roughly, a join of a simplicial set S with the 0-simplex ∆0 consists in adding
a vertex to the simplicial set and all possible higher simplices that contain that
vertex. This construction is also known as the cone over S. This is described in
more detail in [Li14, §2.6.1] and [Joy02].
By observing Figures 1.7, 1.8 and 1.9 with (1.77) in mind, it becomes evident
that the construction is compatible with joins. In fact, while translating simplicial
diagrams to and from the units on the base, all the horn fillings constructing
the “prism” in V′ are “the join” with an extra point of a horn filling in V which
construct a simplicial diagram which is the join of a “prism” with an extra point.
This leads us to expect the same result of Theorem 1.4.21 to hold for all weak
equivalences of higher vector bundles over Lie ∞-groupoids, by abstracting our
argument with the help of the join operation. We plan to make this precise in
future work.
Remark 1.4.23. The same proof can easily be adapted to the case where f is a
simplicial bundle map over an isomorphism of G.

## Page 96


78
Chapter 2
Duals of higher vector spaces
In this chapter we discuss n-duality for simplicial vector spaces. This is the basic
case of a higher vector bundle over a point. Due to the tensor-hom adjunction
in the category of simplicial vector spaces, a canonical candidate for the n-dual
is already given to us by the mapping space construction. The content of this
chapter is an updated version of part of the preprint [RZ24].
Recall that the theory of simplicial vector spaces is equivalent, both on the
nose and up to homotopy, to that of their normalized complexes, as we dis-
cussed in Section 1.3.1. However, by the Eilenberg-Zilber theorem, the normal-
ized complex functor is not an equivalence of monoidal categories on the nose,
but only a monoidal functor up to a canonical homotopy equivalence, given by
the Eilenberg-Zilber and Alexander-Whitney maps. Therefore, to commute the
functor N with the tensor product, one must remember the data given by this
homotopy. This has far-reaching consequences in our theory of duals, as the
Eilenberg-Zilber theorem affects mapping spaces as much as it affects tensor
products, making it a crucial point in our discussion. Therefore, we begin this
chapter by reviewing this classical theorem, and proposing a new reformulation
of it in terms of mapping spaces in Theorem 2.1.4. We then discuss precisely
how the Eilenberg-Zilber theorem causes the order of a tensor product of VS-
groupoids to be the sum of the orders of its factors (at least up to homotopy) in
Theorem 2.1.5. On the other hand, the order of a mapping space depends only
on (and is equal to) the order of the target, as we see in Theorem 2.1.7, which is
a version of Theorem 1.2.10 for simplicial vector spaces.
In the next section we describe the core of the theory of n-duals and n-shifted
simplicial pairings of simplicial vector spaces. We define the n-dual of a simpli-
cial vector space V as the mapping space Vn∗:= Hom(V, BnR), and review the
fact that, by the tensor-hom adjunction, each n-shifted simplicial pairing between
V and W induces two simplicial maps V →Wn∗and W →Vn∗. With this, we
define the canonical n-dual pairing as the pairing whose right induced map is
the identity Vn∗→Vn∗.
We then move to the category of chain complexes, where we define n-shifted
IM-pairings as chain maps from the tensor product of two chain complexes to the
chain complex which is zero at all degrees except for being R in degree n, denoted
by R[−n]. This terminology comes from the condition of infinitesimal multiplica-
tivity, which is another name for the condition that this is a chain map. We define


## Page 97


79
n-shifted duals of chain complexes: for any chain complex E, this is precisely the
mapping space to R[0], then shifted by −n, or equivalently, Hom(E, R[−n]). Im-
portantly, this operation can also be performed internally to the category of non-
negative chain complexes, which requires a truncation at 0 of the mapping space.
Then each n-shifted IM-pairing can be rewritten in terms of induced maps with
target an n-shifted dual.
A central tool in our theory is the construction of the associated IM-pairing
to each simplicial pairing: By applying the normalized complex functor and pre-
composing with the Eilenberg-Zilber map, we can induce an IM-pairing λα :
N(V) ⊗N(W) →R[−n] from any simplicial pairing α : V ⊗W. This allows
us to define the homological nondegeneracy condition on a simplicial pairing: α
is homologically nondegenerate if λα induces a nondegenerate pairing between
the homology groups of the normalized complexes (up to a shift by n). We
then prove what is possibly the main theorem of the theory of n-duals (Theo-
rem 2.2.13): The fact that the canonical n-dual pairing Vn∗⊗V →BnR is ho-
mologically nondegenerate if and only if V is an n-type. A perhaps unfortunate
consequence of having to pass through the Eilenberg-Zilber map is that even in
the case where V is an n-groupoid, the n-dual pairing is only nondegenerate on
the nose for n = 0 and n = 1. In all other cases the n-dual on the simplicial
vector space side contains extra information that vanishes up to homotopy with
respect to the n-shifted dual on the chain complex side. This makes the theory
more complex but perhaps also more interesting.
We conclude this section by discussing two consequences of the main theo-
rem. The first is a characterization of the homological nondegeneracy condition
in terms of a homotopical nondegeneracy condition: in Theorem 2.2.17, we show
that an n-shifted simplicial pairing is homologically nondegenerate if and only if
its induced maps are weak equivalences. The second one is the fact that n-duality
is reflexive for n-types, up to homotopy equivalence: the left induced map of the
canonical pairing is a weak equivalence.
The third section of this chapter is devoted to the explicit computation of n-
duals. We begin with an overview of the equations describing each level of the
n-dual as a space of simplicial maps and introduce the necessary terminology.
After that, we solve the equations for the 1-dual of a VS 1-groupoid and show
that it coincides with the 1-dual VB-groupoid from Section 1.4.1. The main result
of this section is the computation of the 2-dual of a VS 2-groupoid, which pro-
duces a new object. We solve the equations in detail and compute the normalized
complex of this object, to compare it to the 2-shifted dual of the normalized com-
plex of the original space.
We close the chapter with a discussion of a different possible model of a 2-
dual, and that is the simplicial vector space obtained by applying the inverse
DK of the normalized complex functor N to the 2-shifted dual of the normalized
complex of the original space. Mainly we discuss why this route would be prob-
lematic in the case of vector bundles and why we chose not to pursue it. The main
result is that in this case, the canonical 2-dual pairing would be the one obtained
by composing the Alexander-Whitney map with the canonical pairing of chains,
which is different from the canonical evaluation pairing of V2 as a vector space.
In fact, the latter is not multiplicative, and as such does not extend to a simplicial


## Page 98


80
Duals of higher vector spaces
pairing DK(N(V)∗[−2]) ⊗V →B2R.
2.1
The Eilenberg-Zilber theorem revisited
The Eilenberg-Zilber theorem describes how the Dold-Kan correspondence acts
on the tensor products on each side. This originally appeared in [EZ53], [EM54,
Thm. 2.1]. See also [May67, §29], [Lur24, Tags 00RF, 00S0]. More precisely, it
describes a lax monoidal structure and an oplax monoidal structure for the nor-
malized complex functor N, which assemble into a deformation retraction. This
implies that the functor N is strong monoidal between the homotopy categories,
as we summarize in Corollary 2.1.3.
Definition 2.1.1. Let V and W be two simplicial vector spaces. The (normalized)
Eilenberg-Zilber map is the natural transformation of chain complexes
EZ : N(V) ⊗N(W) −→N(V ⊗W)
defined on elementary tensors v ⊗w ∈N(V)p ⊗N(W)q as
EZ(v ⊗w) =
∑
(µ,ν)∈Sh(p,q)
sgn(µ, ν)(sνq . . . sν1v) ⊗(sµp . . . sµ1w),
(2.1)
where Sh(p, q) is the set of (p, q)-shuffles from Definition 1.1.15.
This definition makes use of what is sometimes known as the shuffle product
between chains (see e.g. [Lur24, Tag 00RF]). Additionally, this map can also be
defined in the same way at the level of the Moore complexes, in what is known as
the unnormalized Eilenberg-Zilber map. It can then be observed that it preserves
normalized chains by the simplicial identities.
Proposition 2.1.2 (Normalized Eilenberg-Zilber Theorem [EM54, Thm. 2.1a]).
Let V and W be two simplicial vector spaces. The Eilenberg-Zilber map EZ admits a left
inverse AW, called Alexander-Whitney map,
N(V) ⊗N(W)
EZ
−−→
←−−
AW N(V ⊗W),
such that
AW ◦EZ = idN(V)⊗N(W),
EZ ◦AW ∼idN(V⊗W).
Both of these maps and the homotopy are natural in V and W. In other words, these maps
define a natural chain homotopy equivalence.
Since chain homotopy equivalences are precisely the isomorphisms in the cat-
egory hCh≥0(Vec), we have the following result.
Corollary 2.1.3. The functor N : hSVec →hCh≥0(Vec) is monoidal, that is, for any
simplicial vector spaces V and W,
N(V) ⊗N(W) ≃N(V ⊗W).
Moreover, by the Yoneda embedding, for any simplicial vector spaces U, V and W,
hCh≥0(Vec)(N(U ⊗V), N(W)) ∼= hCh≥0(Vec)(N(U) ⊗N(V), N(W)).

## Page 101


2.1 The Eilenberg-Zilber theorem revisited
83
Order of tensor products and mapping spaces between VS n−groupoids
The fact that the chain homotopy equivalence in the Eilenberg-Zilber Theorem
in Proposition 2.1.2 is even a deformation retract has consequences on the order
and homotopy type of tensor products of VS n-groupoids. The following result
expands on [HT23, Remark 8.5], which observes that the tensor product of two
VS 1-groupoids is not generally a VS 1-groupoid. As the authors of this remark
write, this is in contrast with what [BC04], [Roy07] claimed, in that 2Vec is not a
monoidal category, as it is not closed under the induced monoidal product from
SVec. This fact also influences tensor products of VB n-groupoids. In fact, the
tensor product of two VB 1-groupoids is not generally a 1-groupoid again.
Theorem 2.1.5. Let V be an n-groupoid of order n and W be an m-groupoid of order
m. Then V ⊗W has order at least n + m and homotopy type at most n + m.
Proof. This is because (N(V) ⊗N(W))n+m = N(V)n ⊗N(W)m, since the other
summands vanish by the hypothesis on the order of V and W. For the same
reason, this is the maximal non-zero degree of the tensor product of the nor-
malized complexes. By the Eilenberg-Zilber Theorem 2.1.2, EZ admits a left in-
verse, hence it must be degreewise injective. Therefore EZ((N(V) ⊗N(W))n+m)
is a non-zero subspace of N(V ⊗W)m+n, which must then be non-zero. Since
N(V) ⊗N(W) has amplitude (0, n + m), its homology has at most the same am-
plitude. This is isomorphic to the homology of N(V ⊗W) by the Eilenberg-
Zilber homotopy equivalence, hence the homotopy type of V ⊗W is at most
n + m.
Remark 2.1.6. Theorem 2.1.5 effectively states that, a priori, a tensor product of
a VS n-groupoid with a VS m-groupoid is a groupoid of order higher or equal
to n + m, which is nevertheless homotopy equivalent to an (n + m)-groupoid.
Models of this (n + m)-groupoid can be obtained by DK(H(N(V) ⊗N(W))), as
mentioned in Remark 1.3.21.
A similar argument using the deformation retract in Theorem 2.1.4 can be
used to show that a lower bound on the order of a mapping space depends on
the order of the target (and on a lower bound on the amplitude of the normalized
complex of the source). We are more interested in showing that it is exactly the
order of the target, by adapting to simplicial vector spaces the result of Theorem
1.2.10.
Theorem 2.1.7. Let U be a simplicial vector space, and V be a VS n-groupoid. Then the
mapping space Hom(U, V) is a VS n-groupoid.
Proof. A choice of bases at each level of the simplicial vector space U gives a
simplicial set B such that U ∼= R[B]. Since a linear morphism is determined
by where the base vectors go, we have SVec(U, V) = hom(B, V). Furthermore,
SVec(U ⊗∆m, V) = hom(B × ∆m, V) because a compatible base for (U ⊗∆m)l is
given by Bl × ∆m
l . Thus we have
HomSVec(U, V) = HomSSet(B, V).
(2.3)
Then Lemma 1.2.10 implies that the underlying simplicial set of HomSVec(U, V)
is an n-groupoid. Therefore HomSVec(U, V) is a VS n-groupoid.

In the particular case that V is a 0-groupoid, i.e. the identity groupoid of the

is only nondegenerate up to homotopy, as we will show in Theorem 2.2.13.
In this example we can see that, despite the fact that every simplicial vector
space admits an n-dual for any n ≥0, this might a priori come with a loss of infor-
mation. Our aim in the following discussion is to make such a statement precise
and explain why this happens. The main tools at our disposal are the Dold-Kan
correspondence and the Eilenberg-Zilber Theorem for mapping spaces. Roughly
speaking, the problem lies in the fact that the n-dual pairing is related to the ho-
motopy equivalence EZH, and this sees only a truncated version of the n-shifted
dual in chain complexes. We define the latter object in Definition 2.2.8 and write
this result in Theorem 2.2.13.
On the other side of the Dold-Kan correspondence from simplicial pairings,
we have shifted pairings of chain complexes, which we call IM-pairings, fol-
lowing [CZ23], where the associated IM-form to a shifted 2-form on a Lie n-
groupoid was defined using a formula from [Get]. This terminology is in refer-
ence to the theory of infinitesimally multiplicative forms and tensors appearing
in [BCWZ04], [BC12], [BD19].

negative degrees. An n-shifted IM-pairing λ between A and B is a chain map

As for usual vector spaces, an advantage of considering the maps induced by
an IM-pairing is that they are isomorphisms precisely when the pairing is non-
degenerate. In chain complexes, we can additionally consider nondegeneracy of
a pairing in homology, and this is equivalent to the induced maps being quasi-
isomorphisms. By the following proposition associating an IM-pairing to each
simplicial pairing, this allows to define a notion of nondegeneracy up to homo-
topy for simplicial pairings which we summarize in Definition 2.2.12. This result
appeared previously in [CZ23] for n-shifted 2-forms, where it was interpreted
in [CZ23, Remark 2.15] as an instance of the Van Est map discussed in [AC11,
§6] in relation with IM-forms. The formula (2.15) originally comes from [Get].
Here, we reformulate it for pairings in SVec in terms of the Eilenberg-Zilber map.

degenerate in certain cases, which we now discuss.

Remark 2.2.14. We can see immediately from the proof that if V is a VS n-group-
oid, then the right induced map λr
⟨·,·⟩is exactly EZH. In a sense, this is the best
case scenario, where the information of N(V)∗[−n] does not get lost at all, not
just up to homotopy as in the case of an n-type. To make this statement more pre-
cise we follow the principle that n-dualization corresponds to shifted dualization
on the normalized complexes but the Dold-Kan correspondence only sees non-
negative degrees.
First of all, if V has order m, N(V) has amplitude (0, m), and the n-shifted
dual N(V)∗[−n] has amplitude (n −m, n). If m ≤n, N(V)∗[−n] is non-negative,

2.2 Dual n-groupoids and pairings

and it coincides with the truncation N(V)∗[−n] = N(V)∗[−n]≥0. In this case
n-dualization causes no truncation. On the other hand, if m > n, the amplitude
of N(V)∗[−n]≥0 is strictly smaller than that of N(V)∗[−n], and in particular the
non-negative dual contains less information than N(V). If V is at most an n-type
there is a truncation, but this causes no loss of information up to homotopy as the
homology of N(V) has amplitude smaller than (0, n). Otherwise, the truncation
forgets some of the homology as well. Thus, for m > n, there is generally a
loss of information, which is only avoided up to homotopy for m-groupoids with
homotopy type at most n.
This phenomenon is consistent with the situation for n-shifted symplectic
structures explained in Remark 2.16, Example 2.20, and Example 2.23 of [CZ23].
Remark 2.2.15. When extending this construction to the category of simplicial
vector bundles, with the generalized Dold-Kan correspondence in [HT24], ex-
tra care must be taken. Whatever the n-dual may be in this category, the above
discussion still applies, and the truncation might additionally cause issues with
smoothness and regularity. These might cause the n-dual to not be representable
as a simplicial vector bundle and “fall out of the category”. The simplest exam-
ple of this effect is the 0-dual of a higher vector bundle over a manifold which we
discuss in 3.2.12.
Example 2.2.16. As in Example 2.2.6, the 0-dual pairing for a VS 0-groupoid is
even nondegenerate at the level of spaces, and its IM-pairing is nondegenerate on
chains. As we will show in Remark 2.3.14, the 1-dual pairing for a VS 1-groupoid
is also nondegenerate at the level of spaces, and its IM-pairing is nondegenerate
on chains. The situation changes entirely for the 2-dual pairing, as we will see
in Proposition 2.3.19, since this is only homologically nondegenerate even for VS
2-groupoids. This can be interpreted as the 2-dual containing additional infor-
mation that is redundant up to homotopy.

Remark 2.2.18. In the setting of the theorem, if either V or W is at most an n-
type and α is homologically nondegenerate, then the other is also at most an
n-type because it is weak equivalent to a VS n-groupoid. This puts restrictions
on which simplicial vector spaces admit homologically nondegenerate pairings
with respect to a certain shift. This is analogous to the fact discussed in [CZ23,
Remark 2.16] that an m-shifted symplectic Lie n-groupoid with m < n must have
certain vanishing homology groups, which make it interpretable as an m-shifted
symplectic Lie m-groupoid with added singularities.
Remark 2.2.19. A trivial but perhaps illustrative fact to observe is that in the
case of the n-dual pairing α = ⟨·, ·⟩: Vn∗⊗V →BnR the right-hand diagram in
(2.16) becomes tautological, and the theorem only reiterates the fact that if V is
an n-type to begin with, then λr
⟨·,·⟩is a quasi-isomorphism and the n-dual pairing
is homologically nondegenerate.
On the contrary, the left-hand diagram yields an interesting property which
deserves to be a theorem of its own.
Theorem 2.2.20 (n-duality is reflexive up to homotopy). Let V be at most an n-type.
The double n-dual (Vn∗)n∗is weak equivalent to V itself. That is
⟨·, ·⟩l : V →(Vn∗)n∗
(2.17)
is a weak equivalence.
Proof. By Theorem 2.2.17 and Theorem 2.2.13, the left induced map in Example
2.2.5 is a weak equivalence.
Remark 2.2.21. In SVec, by applying Proposition 1.3.20, if V is an n-type, then
⟨·, ·⟩l : V →(Vn∗)n∗is even a homotopy equivalence.

2.3
Computing n-duals of simplicial vector spaces
To calculate explicitly what the n-dual (2.8) is involves solving many linear equa-
tions. Determining the solution space of these linear equations for a general n is
not a trivial task due to the large number of equations involved. In this section
we give an overview of the equations for a general n and set out to solve them in
the case of a VS n-groupoid for n = 1 and n = 2. For n = 1, we rediscover the
dual of VB groupoids from Definition 1.4.11 applied to the case where the base
is a point. For n = 2, we discover a new object.
2.3.1
Overview of the general computation for the n-dual of a simpli-
cial vector space

compute Vn∗for a general n. Since Vn∗is a VS n-groupoid, its full data is con-
tained in its n-truncation, by Proposition 1.3.23. Even though the multiplications
of its n-simplices can be recovered by computing Moore fillers, these have a nice
description in terms of the n-dual pairing, which we later discuss.

equations (2.19). As we will see, these generalize the different isomorphic de-
scriptions of the dual of the core of a VB 1-groupoid we discussed in Section
1.4.1.

We now focus on the two extreme cases needed for computing 1- and 2-duals,
which we use in Section 2.3.3 and Section 2.3.4. These are degree 1 n-dimensional
cores and degree n n-dimensional cores. As we will show, in these cases, the
degeneracy annihilators are isomorphic to the duals of the cores of the corre-
sponding degree. We comment on a possible way to investigate this for degree

Since different annihilators appear separately as solution spaces of the nor-

p ∈M. In particular, the restriction of any VB groupoid V →G to the units G0 is
such a VB groupoid, and its fiber at any p ∈G0 is exactly the VS 1-dual (V|p)1∗
of the fiber of V at p. In other words:

2.3.5
The normalized complex of the 2-dual
We compute here the normalized complex of the 2-dual for future reference. Re-
call that for the 1-dual of a VS 1-groupoid, as computed in Remark 2.3.14, we
have an isomorphism N(V1∗) ∼= N(V)∗[−1]. On the contrary, in the case of a
VS 2-groupoid V, N(V2∗) is only chain homotopy equivalent to N(V)∗[−2], as a
consequence of Theorem 2.1.4 and Theorem 2.2.13. The case of n = 2 is indeed
the first case in increasing order of n where the n-dual pairing of a VS n-groupoid
is only homologically nondegenerate and not nondegenerate on the nose.

2.4
The model DK(N(V)∗[−n])
As anticipated in Remark 2.2.10, we now discuss the other possible model of
n-dual imported from chain complexes via the Dold-Kan correspondence. For
simplicity, throughout this section, whenever we talk about the n-dual of V we
consider V to be a VS n-groupoid.
First of all, DK(N(V)∗[−n]) is the n-dual of the VS n-groupoid V for n = 0, 1
because in these cases the deformation retract in Theorem 2.1.4 is actually an
isomorphism, as in Example 2.2.6 and Remark 2.3.14. In these cases the n-dual
pairing is also nondegenerate on the nose and it coincides with a trivial extension
of the canonical evaluation pairing of the n-th level Vn with its dual vector space
to a simplicial pairing.
In general cases (for n ≥2), we would like to retain this property of the n-dual
pairing being a trivial extension of the evaluation pairing of V∗
n to a canonical
simplicial pairing Vn∗⊗V →BnR, while requiring it to be nondegenerate only
up to homotopy. This is precisely what we get, as we saw in Section 2.3.1. On the
other hand, using the maps in the proof of Theorem 2.1.4, one could produce a
pairing on DK(N(V)∗[−n]) by taking the evaluation pairing on chains, which is
a chain map λ : N(V)∗[−n] ⊗N(V) →R[−n], and defining DK(AW∗λ). There
are two reasons why this is inconvenient:
Firstly, although at level n, DK(N(V)∗[−n])n ∼= V∗
n, the pairing DK(AW∗λ)
will not coincide with the evaluation pairing ev : V∗
n ⊗Vn →R outside the cases
n = 0, 1. This is because, as we show in Proposition 2.4.2, for n ≥2, ev does
not extend trivially to a simplicial pairing DK(N(V)∗[−n]) ⊗V →Bn(R) even
in simple cases.
The second reason has to do with the version of this theory for higher vector
bundles. As shown in [HT24], the Dold-Kan correspondence for higher vec-
tor bundles involves the extra data given by a representation up to homotopy
(RUTH) of the base simplicial manifold on the normalized complex of the higher
vector bundle in question. While the construction of the chain complex N is
entirely analogous to that for simplicial vector spaces, a choice of a cleavage is
needed to obtain the RUTH. In addition, a RUTH of a Lie n-groupoid G for n ≥2
cannot be canonically dualized to a RUTH of G, but only to a RUTH of its opposite
Gop obtained by simplicial front-to-back symmetry as in Remark 1.1.11. This is
due to the absence of an inversion map for arrows in Lie n-groupoids with n ≥2
which usually gives the canonical isomorphism between a Lie groupoid and its
opposite. The RUTH is also essential to define the functor DK, and applying this
functor to a RUTH of Gop gives a higher vector bundle on Gop. Therefore, in this

## Page 130

Chapter 3
Duals of VB n-groupoids
In this chapter we define the n-dual of any higher vector bundle over a Lie n-
groupoid by means of a universal property. As we will see, this is obtained at
each level by a categorical limit in the category of vector bundles, which means
its existence is not guaranteed, but depends on the existence and regularity of
solutions for a certain set of equations.
We begin by defining n-shifted pairings for higher vector bundles over the
same base in Section 3.1. As for vector spaces, these are pairings with values in
the Eilenberg-MacLane space BnR, this time seen as a trivial simplicial vector
bundle over the base Lie n-groupoid. We then define the associated IM-pairings
and the homological nondegeneracy condition. This condition previously ap-
peared for shifted symplectic structures on a Lie groupoid. We recall their defi-
nition here, as well.
Section 3.2 deals with the general construction of n-duals of higher vector
bundles over a Lie n-groupoid starting with the universal property. We then
develop some notation to implement the equations in Section 2.3.1 pointwise over
the base Lie n-groupoid and show that these identify each level of the n-dual
as a limit in VB in Theorem 3.2.9. At this point we show that the VB n-dual is
consistent with the VS n-dual, and that it can be constructed by solving only a
finite number of equations. In the next subsection we discuss some properties of
the n-dual pairing that carry over from the vector space version, i.e. the fact that
it is homologically n-shifted nondegenerate in Theorem 3.2.15 and its relation to
the induced map of the IM-pairing of any other n-shifted pairing in Theorem
3.2.16.
After that, we review the terminology necessary to discuss solution spaces of
the n-dual equations by generalizing the notion of cores of a VB groupoid. We
close this section by solving the 1-dual equations pointwise for a VB 1-groupoid
and showing that this recovers the VB 1-dual from Section 1.4.1. We then recall
the analog of Theorem 2.2.17 in this case: by using the 1-dual, one can refor-
mulate the homological nondegeneracy of a 1-shifted pairing as the fact that its
induced maps are weak equivalences.
In Section 3.3 we define a model of the 2-dual of a VB 2-groupoid from the top
down and show this is a VB 2-groupoid by checking the finite data conditions in
Example 1.2.69, and that it satisfies the 2-dual equations. We then apply Theorem
1.4.21 to Theorem 3.2.16 and obtain the VB 2-dual version of Theorem 2.2.17: a


## Page 131


3.1 Pairings of higher vector bundles
113
2-shifted pairing is homologically nondegenerate if and only if its induced maps
are weak equivalences. Applied to the 2-dual pairing, this means that 2-duality is
reflexive up to homotopy for VB 2-groupoids. In the final section of this chapter,
we compute 2-duals of vector bundles and VB 1-groupoids explicitly.

property. This is the target space for simplicial pairings of higher vector bundles.

the same name.

An important example of homologically nondegenerate n-shifted pairings is
given by n-shifted symplectic structures on Lie m-groupoids. These were intro-
duced in [Get] and studied in [CZ23]. We give a slightly different but equivalent
definition that follows naturally from this discussion. See [CZ23] for more de-
tails.
Definition 3.1.11. Let G be a Lie m-groupoid. An n-shifted presymplectic form
on G is a collection of differential forms
(ω2, ω3, . . . , ωn+2), with ωi ∈Ωi(Gn−i+2),
such that
1. ω2 ∈Ω2(Gn) induces a simplicial pairing TG ⊗TG →B2RG, i.e. it satisfies
the multiplicativity and normalization conditions (3.1) and (3.2).2
1By the same argument as in Definition 2.2.12, if one is a quasi-isomorphism at each point, the
other one is as well.
2Since ω2 is a 2-form, the pairing it induces is in particular antisymmetric (at level n).

2. For any i, dωi = (−1)n−i+1δωi+1, where δ is the simplicial differential (3.3),
and d is the de Rham differential.
3. For any i, ωi is normalized in the sense that s∗
j ωi = 0 ∈Ωi(Gn−i+1).
If, in addition, the pairing induced by ω2 is homologically nondegenerate,
then we say ω is an n-shifted symplectic form.
In practice, in this thesis, we will only consider shifted symplectic forms for
which the only non-zero component is ω2. In particular, for such structures,
dω2 = 0.
Remark 3.1.12. In (2) it is intended that δω2 = 0, which is also contained in
the first condition (multiplicativity of the pairing), and that dωn+2 = 0. In (3)
normalization of ω2 is equivalent to the fact it is normalized as a pairing, so this
condition is already included in (1).
Condition (2) and the multiplicativity of condition (1) are equivalent to the
fact that ω is closed with respect to the total differential δ + (−1)pd (p is the sim-
plicial degree) in the Bott-Shulman-Stasheff double complex Ω•(G•) introduced
in [BSS76]. The collection of forms (ωi)i is an element of degree n + 2 in the to-
tal complex. It is required to have vanishing components in bidegrees (0, n + 2)
and (1, n + 1) for reasons explained in [Get, p.27], which originally come from
[PTVV13].
3.2
General construction of VB n-duals
We first give a definition of the n-dual by a universal property.
Definition 3.2.1. Let V be a higher vector bundle over a Lie n-groupoid G. If it
exists, the n-dual of V is the representing object of the functor
VB∞
G (V ⊗_, BnRG) :(VB∞
G )op →Vec
W →VB∞
G (V ⊗W, BnRG),
from the category of higher vector bundles over G with simplicial bundle maps
over the identity of G to the category of vector spaces. If it exists, we denote the
n-dual of V by Vn∗.
In the following, we show that the n-dual of a higher vector bundle can be de-
scribed as a limit, by identifying it as the kernel of a bundle map, i.e. a simplicial
subbundle of a certain ambient bundle satisfying a series of equations which are
a pointwise version of the ones in Proposition 2.3.1. Because this construction is
a limit, a priori this still does not solve the question of existence, which has three
obstructions:
1. The equations need to admit solutions at each fiber and each level.
2. The solution spaces must assemble into levelwise constant rank vector bun-
dles and define a simplicial vector bundle. See Example 3.2.12 for an exam-
ple of a situation in which this fails to hold.


## Page 135


3.2 General construction of VB n-duals
117
3. The result satisfies the appropriate Kan conditions, so that it is indeed a
higher vector bundle.
By existence of dual vector bundles and dual VB groupoids, we know that these
requirements are satisfied in the case of the n-dual of a VB n-groupoid for n =
0, 1. We will then show these are also satisfied in the case of n = 2. In any case,
because of the defining property being universal, if any such solution exists, it is
unique up to isomorphism, and we can already use its definition to study some
of its properties.

## Page 141


3.2 General construction of VB n-duals
123
We now discuss two properties of the n-dual of a higher vector bundle that
can be directly inferred from the n-dual equations from Lemma 3.2.4. The first
one is that this construction is consistent with the n-dual of a simplicial vector
space, in the sense that the pullback to the units of the n-dual is pointwise the
n-dual of the pullback to the units. This allows us to make some remarks about
existence of 0-duals of higher vector bundles over a 0-groupoid.
The second one allows us to show that given a candidate VB n-groupoid
whose finite data satisfies the n-dual equations from Lemma 3.2.4 for levels 0 ≤
m ≤n + 1, this satisfies all higher equations as well, and therefore it is the n-dual.
In the discussion of the VS n-duals we obtained a similar result on the finiteness
of equations in the opposite way: we first showed that the VS n-dual is always a
VS n-groupoid and then because of that it was determined by its finite data. In
the VB case we need to show the proposed finite data solving the equations is in
fact a VB n-groupoid by other means, as we do for n = 2 in Theorem 3.3.7.
Lemma 3.2.11. Let V →G be a higher vector bundle over a Lie n-groupoid G. If its
n-dual Vn∗exists, then
(1∗Vn∗)|p ∼= ((1∗V)|p)n∗,
as simplicial vector spaces. That is, the fiber of the pullback of the n-dual of V by the total
unit map at any point p ∈G0 is the VS n-dual of the fiber of the pullback of V by the
unit map, at that same point.
Proof. Recall from Remark 3.2.3 that the simplicial fiber of V at a unit 1p ∈Gm is
R (1p)∗V ∼= (1∗V)|p ⊗∆m. Therefore, for any m ≥0,
(1∗Vn∗)m|p ∼= SVec((1∗V)|p ⊗∆m, BnR) = ((1∗V)|p)n∗
m .
Moreover, the simplicial maps induced on 1∗Vn∗by 1∗AnV are the same as the
simplicial maps of the VS n-dual (compare (1.18) with (3.13) and (3.14)). So
these levelwise isomorphisms upgrade to an isomorphism of simplicial vector
spaces.
This result gives an immediate example of a situation where the n-dual of a
higher vector bundle does not exist.
Remark 3.2.12. Because of this lemma, if V is a higher vector bundle over a 0-
groupoid M, its n-dual can be computed pointwise at each p ∈M by using the
construction of the VS n-dual in Section 2.3. In general, without any assumptions
on the order of V, the pointwise solution spaces might not assemble to vector
bundles over M at each level.
Consider for example the 0-dual of a VB n-groupoid over a 0-groupoid M. By
Example 2.2.6, we have
V0∗
l |p ∼= H0(V|p)∗,
for any l ≥0 and any p ∈M. This homology is generally not constant rank
over M and as such, V0∗is not always a well-defined VB 0-groupoid. On the
other hand, if V is a VB 0-groupoid over M (i.e. a classical vector bundle), then
this construction coincides with the usual dual of vector bundles and it is well-
defined.

We conclude this section by showing that the n-dual can be computed by solv-
ing a finite number of equations.
Lemma 3.2.13. Let V →G be a higher vector bundle over a Lie n-groupoid. Then,
the ambient n-dual bundle AnV is n-coskeletal. Moreover, let V′ →G be a simplicial
subbundle of AnV. The following are equivalent
(i) V′ satisfies the n-dual equations of Lemma 3.2.4 at all levels m ≥0.
(ii) V′ satisfies the n-dual equations at levels 0 ≤m ≤n + 1 and it is (n + 1)-
coskeletal.
In particular any simplicial subbundle of AnV that is a VB n-groupoid whose finite data
satisfies the n-dual equations for V is the n-dual Vn∗.

By definition of τ, the n-dual pairing is a simplicial pairing, so it satisfies the
multiplicativity and normalization conditions (3.1), (3.2). The construction of
the associated IM-pairing to a pairing of higher vector bundles happens point-
wise over G0, and the condition of homological nondegeneracy for such a pairing
is also required to hold pointwise over G0, by Definitions 3.1.9 and 3.1.10. There-
fore, the following two properties of the n-dual pairing of higher vector bundles
follow immediately from their respective version for simplicial vector spaces dis-
cussed in Theorem 2.2.13 and 2.2.17.
Note that at this point we only have the latter part of Theorem 2.2.17 for VB n-
groupoids with n ≤2, as the correspondence between weak equivalences of VB
n-groupoids over the identity map of the base and quasi-isomorphisms of their
normalized complexes was only established for n = 1 in [HO20, Thm 3.5], and
we extended it to n = 2 in Theorem 1.4.21. We will discuss this in the sections
relative to the 1-dual and the 2-dual.

orem 1.4.21 in the case of VB 1-groupoids, which was shown in [HO20, Thm.
3.5]. We begin with recalling the simplicial maps induced by a 1-shifted simpli-
cial pairing. Let α : V1 ⊗W1 →R × G1 be a multiplicative normalized 1-shifted
pairing between two VB 1-groupoids over G. Then, by Remark 3.2.10, α induces
the maps

Remark 3.2.22. By (3.28), the 1-dual pairing ⟨·, ·⟩induces two isomorphisms:
⟨·, ·⟩r = id : V1∗→V1∗,

In fact, assuming V is a VB 1-groupoid, the 1-dual pairing is not only homolog-
ically nondegenerate, but the induced IM-pairing λ⟨·,·⟩is even nondegenerate at
the level of chains. This is a peculiarity of the n = 0 and n = 1 cases of n-duals
of VB n-groupoids. In all other cases the n-dual pairing is a priori only nondege-
nerate in the homology, and one should not expect the n-duality to be reflexive
on the nose. We will show in Corollary 3.3.10 that 2-duality of VB 2-groupoids
is reflexive up to homotopy, as n-duality of VS n-types is, by Theorem 2.2.20. A
more general reflexivity result of n-duality for general VB n-groupoids would be
immediate after extending Theorem 1.4.21 to any n. We plan to complete this in
future work.
We conclude with a characterization of homologically nondegenerate 1-shifted
simplicial pairings in terms of weak equivalences (i.e. Morita maps in the termi-
nology of [HO20]).
Corollary 3.2.23. Let α : V1 ⊗W1 →R × G1 be a multiplicative normalized 1-shifted
pairing between two VB 1-groupoids over G. Then the following are equivalent.
(i) α is homologically nondegenerate.
(ii) αr is a weak equivalence.

Remark 3.3.5. Recall that in the VS case we were able to express the conditions
corresponding to (3.32) and (3.33) in terms of any 2-simplex in V, as in (2.39).
In this case, a generic 2-simplex Xt ∈(V2)t does not canonically project down to
ker p2
j as before, since the degeneracies of its faces do not live in the same fiber
of V2 over t and thus cannot be subtracted to it. This, combined with the fact
that only certain translations over degeneracies are allowed a priori, forces us to
make some choices of specific kernels to make fiber products over, while in the
VS case we could do everything only with annihilators.
Because the definitions of each component ϕ002, ϕ022, and ϕ011 through (3.36),
(3.37), and (3.38), include choices, we need to check that they are well-posed and
independent of choices. We show this in the following lemma, which includes
some convenient identities as well.

The following two results show that the data above is actually the 2-dual of
the VB 2-groupoid V.
Theorem 3.3.7. The finite data in Definition 3.3.1 defines a VB 2-groupoid.
As the proof of this theorem is a series of lengthy computations to check that
the data in Definition 3.3.1 satisfies the conditions of Example 1.2.69, we leave it
to Section 3.3.1. We now proceed to showing that V2∗as defined above satisfies
the 2-dual equations from Lemma 3.2.4.

We conclude with a characterization of homologically nondegenerate pair-
ings that follows from Theorem 1.4.21. This can be applied to show that a 2-
shifted presymplectic form on a Lie 2-groupoid is 2-shifted symplectic if it in-
duces a Morita equivalence between the tangent and the 2-cotangent of the Lie
2-groupoid. With this we can also show that 2-duality of VB 2-groupoids is re-
flexive up to homotopy.
We begin by writing explicitly the maps induced by a 2-shifted simplicial pair-
ing as defined in Remark 3.2.10. Let α : V2 ⊗W2 →R be a multiplicative nor-
malized 2-shifted pairing between two VB 2-groupoids over G. Then α induces
the maps
αr : V →W2∗,
αl : W →V2∗,
where αr is defined as
αr
2(Xt) = (α(Xt, _), (α(es0 ed0Xt, _), α(es1 ed0Xt, _)), α(es0 ed2Xt, _)),
αr
1(vg) = (α(es0vg, _), α(es1vg, _)),
αr
0(xp) = α(e1xp, _),
(3.50)
for any Xt ∈V2|t, vg ∈V1|g and xp ∈V0|p. The other map αl is defined by
inserting elements of W in α in a similar way.
Corollary 3.3.9. Let α : V2 ⊗W2 →R × G2 be a multiplicative normalized 2-shifted
pairing between two VB 2-groupoids over G. Then the following are equivalent:
(i) α is homologically 2-shifted nondegenerate.
(ii) αr is a weak equivalence.
(iii) αl is a weak equivalence.
In particular, if ω2 is the leading term of a 2-shifted presymplectic form on a Lie 2-
groupoid G, it is 2-shifted symplectic if and only if
ωr
2 = −ωl
2 : TG →T2∗G
is a weak equivalence.
Proof. This follows immediately from Theorem 1.4.21 and commutativity of the
diagram (3.20) from Theorem 3.2.16, where the 2-dual pairing is homologically
2-shifted nondegenerate by Theorem 3.2.15. In this situation the induced chain
map λr
α is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if N(αr) is a quasi-isomorphism, and
the latter is equivalent to αr being a weak equivalence.
With this result and the fact that the 2-dual pairing of a VB 2-groupoid is
homologically nondegenerate by Theorem 3.2.15, we have the VB version of re-
flexivity up to homotopy from Theorem 2.2.20 for n = 2.
Corollary 3.3.10. The double 2-dual (V2∗)2∗of a VB 2-groupoid V →G is weak equiv-
alent to V, via the left induced map of the 2-dual pairing.

3.4
Examples of VB n-duals
We conclude this chapter by presenting some cases of n-duals for n ≤2 for VB
groupoids of order less than 2, where the general formulas simplify. The case of
the 0-dual of a VB n-groupoid over a manifold was considered in Remark 3.2.12.
Example 3.4.1 (1- and 2- dual of a VB 0-groupoid). The most trivial example of a
higher vector bundle is that of a VB 0-groupoid, which is just the unit groupoid of
a vector bundle E →M, for M a smooth manifold. By the discussion in 3.2.12 this
is the only order that always admits a 0-dual, which is just the unit VB-groupoid
of the dual vector bundle E∗→M. For higher duals, observe that, in this case,
all the epm
j are isomorphisms for m ≥1, 0 ≤j ≤m, where ep1
j = ed1
j . Therefore, all
the various kernels present in higher duals vanish, and we have that the 1-dual
is given by the finite data

These results are consistent with the pointwise interpretation from 3.2.11,
where a VB n-groupoid over a 0-groupoid is defined pointwise for each fiber,
as each point p of M has an associated VS n-groupoid (1∗E)p. Then the fiber of
the n-dual at each point is given by the n-dual of this vector space groupoid.

## Page 178


160
Chapter 4
2-cotangents and shifted
symplectic structures
In this short chapter we study the 2-cotangent of a Lie 2-groupoid. Most impor-
tantly, we show that it carries a natural 2-shifted symplectic structure.
When the Lie 2-groupoid is given by the nerve of a Lie groupoid G, we show
how the 2-cotangent T2∗G is Morita equivalent to the 2-groupoid Ę
T1∗G obtained
by using the Artin-Mazur bar construction on the 1-cotangent of G, as we show
in Theorem 4.2.6. The Morita equivalence we construct is also compatible with
the 2-shifted symplectic structures, as we show in Theorem 4.2.7.
The symplectic 2-groupoid Ę
T1∗G was proposed in [MT11] as the global object
integrating the Courant algebroid A ⊕A∗(see [LWX97]). This is meant in the
sense that the tangent complex of Ę
T1∗G is isomorphic (up to a splitting) to the
chain complex
T∗G0 →A ⊕A∗→TG0,
(4.1)
which is the chain complex obtained from A ⊕A∗by taking the anchor and its
dual as differentials. The integration problem for Courant algebroids has been
approached in many ways since Ševera put forward the idea that Courant alge-
broids should integrate to some version of symplectic Lie 2-groupoids in [Šev05].
Since the integration proposed there produces infinite-dimensional groupoids,
the search for finite-dimensional models of integrations has produced various
results in different contexts. We point the interested reader to the introductions
of [MT18a] and the more recent [CZ23], which put the approaches in [LŠ12;
MT11; MT18b; Šev05; ŠŠ19; SZ17] in context.
Under the correspondence in [Roy02], the Courant algebroid A ⊕A∗corre-
sponds to the 2-shifted cotangent degree 2 dg-manifold T∗[2]A[1] of the Lie alge-
broid A considered as a degree 1 dg-manifold. By the symplectic Morita equiv-
alence in 4.2.7, the tangent complex of the 2-cotangent T2∗G is quasi-isomorphic
to the tangent complex of Ę
T1∗G, which is (4.1). In this sense we can think of the
2-cotangent of a Lie groupoid G to be an integration up to homotopy of the 2-shifted
cotangent of its Lie algebroid. This is to say that the operation of taking the 2-
shifted cotangent of a Lie algebroid corresponds up to homotopy to the operation
of taking the 2-cotangent of a Lie groupoid, under a (partial) analog of the Lie
correspondence.

Definition 4.1.2. A simplicial double vector bundle (simplicial DVB for short),
is a double object in the category of simplicial vector bundles. That is, a simplicial
DVB over a simplicial manifold G is a triple (D, A, B) of simplicial manifolds,
where A and B are simplicial vector bundles over G, and D has two compatible
simplicial vector bundle structures over A and B, respectively. The compatibility
condition is that for each m, (Dm, Am, Bm) is a DVB over Gm and the simplicial
maps of D are double vector bundle maps. We represent (D, A, B) by

4.2
The 2-cotangent of a 1-groupoid
In this section we consider the case of a Lie 1-groupoid G. In this case there
are two versions of a cotangent 2-groupoid we can associate to G. One is the
2-cotangent T2∗G and the other is the Artin-Mazur bar T1∗G of the 1-cotangent
T1∗G considered as a double groupoid. (Any simplicial vector bundle can be
seen as a bisimplicial object where one direction is the nerve of its vector bundle
structure.) We refer to [MT11, §2.2] for the bar construction. We will show that
these two “versions” of a 2-cotangent space for G are Morita equivalent. To begin
with, we write down both of these objects explicitly. As usual, in order to avoid
having to keep track of too many inversions, we differentiate explicitly between
the two versions of the Lie algebroid of G: AR, defined as the right core of TG
and AL, defined as the left core thereof.
Another reason why we prefer to use the left Lie algebroid AL in defining
T1∗G is that this allows the Morita equivalence Ξ in (4.24) to be expressed in
terms of an Ehresmann connection splitting the core sequence for AR as in the
more usual convention used in [AC13].
Following Example 3.4.2, the 2-cotangent T2∗G is given by
